#2969. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2960. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2955. Anthropic's trillion-dollar IPO is coming, but retail investors could have owned AI stock much earlier

Anthropic is set to go public next week, with a potential date of Nov. 19 for an initial public offering that could value the AI giant on a fully diluted basis at over $1.4 trillion. For many retail investors, the IPO may be the first chance they have to own Anthropic and even that ownership event will be out of reach with limited shares allocated to retail brokerage firms. But there were ways for retail investors to own Anthropic shares at a much earlier date. Investors may also be able to get in on the next hot IPO well before listing day.

There are several ways to do this, though it can depend on whether you're an accredited investor under SEC guidelines when investing directly in a private company versus accessing a privately held stock through a diversified fund. Generally, this means being able to prove you have $200,000 in individual income, or $300,000 jointly, or $1 million in net worth, not counting an investor's home. The SEC has proposed expanding accredited investor eligibility beyond traditional income and net worth thresholds, through an exam-based route, but that's not currently an option.

Here's what investors looking for some pre-IPO action need to know:

Some mutual funds and ETFs invest in private companies before they are public. "It's not like buying the IPO directly, so you have to be comfortable with the portfolio," said Chester Spatt, professor of finance at Carnegie Mellon University's Tepper School of Business.

That means reviewing all of the portfolio holdings of a fund and its investment approach, not just the fact that it has a stake in the one company that has yet to go public. Even so, mutual funds and ETFs can be a way to gain some exposure to the pre-IPO world, with SpaceX, Anthropic and its rival OpenAI all recent examples.

For example, Fidelity Contrafund, a large-cap growth fund, says in its prospectus that the adviser may invest the fund's assets in securities of private or newly public companies. It was an early investor in SpaceX, for example, buying shares in 2015 when the company was valued at about $10 billion. SpaceX represented about 4.4% of the fund's holdings at the end of September.

Billionaire mutual fund manager Ron Baron's Partners Fund, which seeks long-term capital appreciation, also invested in SpaceX. At the end of October, SpaceX was the fund's No. 1 holding, accounting for 28% of its assets.

Before buying a fund that invests in private companies, be sure to consider redemption and fees, Spatt said. For example, The Private Shares Fund, which seeks capital appreciation by focusing on investments in late-stage, venture-backed private companies, allows quarterly tenders for up to 5% of the fund's outstanding shares at NAV. The management fee is 1.9%, higher than many index funds.

Several ETFs list Anthropic among their holdings, including the Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR), Tema Electrification ETF (VOLT),  Alger Concentrated Equity ETF (CNEQ), Alger AI Enablers & Adopters ETF (ALAI), Alger 35 ETF (ATFV), KraneShares Public-Private AI & Technology ETF (AGIX), and iShares A.I. Innovation and Tech Active ETF (BAI).

Several big mutual fund and ETF companies, similar to the SpaceX shares, have reported ownership in Anthropic in the past, including T. Rowe Price, Capital Group and Fidelity Investments. Fifty Fidelity funds have stakes in Anthropic, according to Morningstar, at roughly $5 billion. Capital Group has an even greater stake across its funds, at $10 billion, according to Morningstar.

But there is a catch: not all the funds own shares outright, with some ETFs gaining exposure through what are called special purpose vehicles.

Fund managers and accredited investors can access late-stage start-ups through special purpose vehicles.

Tema's LAZR ETF, for example, lists Anthropic as its second-biggest holding, but it is through an SPV. Its VOLT fund has a much smaller holding in Anthropic.

Accredited investors can also access SPV-related investment opportunities directly, through an online platform like Caplight, Sydecar and Augment, said Emily Zheng, senior venture capital analyst at private investment research firm Pitchbook, or through their wealth manager. Keep in mind, however, you could be buying an SPV that owns the shares outright, or you could be invested in an SPV that has shares of an SPV that owns the shares, she said. There can also be multiple layers of fees.

"This structure may allow investors to participate at lower minimums than would typically be required for a direct share purchase, while the fund manager handles the complexity of acquisition, ongoing administration, and tax reporting on behalf of investors," according to private wealth and alternative investments manager TSG Invest, which invests in pre-IPO shares. It says SPVs are becoming the dominant structure in pre-IPO investing, now accounting for roughly 40% to 50% of all secondary market transactions.

Accredited investors who want access to shares of private companies that may not be imminently ready to IPO can buy through online private stock trading platforms such as Schwab's Forge Global, Morgan Stanley's EquityZen, Notice, Caplight Technologies and Clarity, previously known as Hiive.

Platforms have different private holdings available, and investment requirements can vary. There can also be multiple fees. For instance, at Forge, opening an account and using the marketplace to trade shares are free. However, buyers pay a one-time fee for both direct shares and fund offerings. Investors should also be aware that some companies may charge their own transfer or legal fees, according to a Forge spokesperson.

Several platforms require investment minimums. The standard minimum for direct secondary transactions at Forge, for example, is $100,000, though minimums may vary by investment type and offering. Certain Forge fund offerings may accept indications of interest starting at $5,000, but availability is limited and subject to the terms of the specific offering, the company says on its website.

Investors need to consider their time horizon and their liquidity needs before investing in private companies. Additionally, minimums at online platforms can be high, even for accredited investors, said Zheng. "But it is a lot lower than traditional venture investment," Zheng said.

Individual investors can get access to private companies through about two dozen publicly available closed-end funds focused on private equity or venture capital, according to Morningstar Direct.

Funds vary in their holdings between early-stage and late-stage companies. OpenAI, for instance, represents about 9.4% of the holdings of Robinhood Ventures Fund I. The fund's holdings also include 4.2% of Ramp, which could IPO at some point in the next year, and its largest holding is enterprise tech company Databricks.

Sofi, meanwhile, offers access to certain funds that may not be available through other brokerages. Private and venture funds on Sofi's platform include the ARK Venture Fund, which owns both OpenAI and Anthropic; and the Destiny Tech100 Closed End Fund, which also owns both AI companies.

Closed-end funds also offer exposure to other closely watched private names, such as the Cashmere Fund, which owns Kalshi. The Fundrise Innovation Fund has reported holdings in Anthropic, OpenAI, Databricks, Anduril and Ramp as of its year-end 2025 statement, but not more recently.

These funds can carry significantly higher fees than index funds or ETFs consisting of public company stocks. Robinhood Ventures Fund I, for example, has an expense ratio of 3.13%.

If a retail investor does want to get in on the IPO, some brokerage firms are able to offer Anthropic IPO access to clients, and you generally don't have to be an accredited investor to be eligible.

Anthropic, for example, said in a Nov. 2 regulatory filing that it was allocating some IPO shares to brokerage customers of Charles Schwab, Fidelity, Robinhood Financial (including its TradePMR division), SoFi, and Morgan Stanley's E-Trade.

SpaceX offered about 20% of its IPO shares to individual investors through these brokerages.

Individual brokerages determine the requirements for participation. SoFi, for example, requires investors to have a particular account type, but there are no account minimums. Fidelity allows access to brokerage customers with at least $25,000 in retail assets at the firm.

To request shares, eligible investors generally enter an indication of interest for the offering they are interested in. However, there's no guarantee investors will receive shares or how many. In many cases, investors may be disappointed with their allocation.

"We only get a limited number of shares for each IPO," Robinhood says on its website. "If customer demand is higher than our supply, we can't fulfill every request in full. How shares are distributed depends on the allocation method used for the offering."

#2947. House Oversight calls on DOJ to interview key Epstein witnesses, says govt. ‘failed’ survivors

The House Oversight and Government Reform Committee on Tuesday released a sharply critical report on the federal government's handling of investigations into Jeffrey Epstein and his accomplice, Ghislaine Maxwell.

"The federal government has failed the survivors of Jeffrey Epstein and Ghislaine Maxwell's horrific crimes for decades," Oversight Chair Rep. James Comer, R-Ky., said in a statement.

The report called for the Justice Department to interview or reinterview "key witnesses" with information about Epstein's sex trafficking of young women and girls, "further investigate allegations of criminal activity involving Epstein's associates, and provide survivors with unredacted access to their records."

The report also urged Congress to pass legislation sponsored by Comer that would allow survivors of sexual abuse to speak freely about it, even if they had signed confidentiality agreements.

In addition to criticizing the federal government, the committee blasted various financial institutions for their roles in dealing with Epstein, who killed himself in August 2019 in jail after being arrested on child sex trafficking charges.

"U.S. and foreign government authorities failed to identify indicators of human trafficking and immigration fraud," the committee said. "Institutions such as American Express enabled this activity, with company agents allowing Epstein to use fraudulent decoy flights and refundable or dummy itineraries to transport foreign women across borders directly to his residences."

"Financial institutions possessed substantial information about Epstein's criminal history and repeatedly identified transactions and activities presenting heightened compliance risks," the committee said. "Yet they failed to adequately address those risks, in some cases reporting them years later after Epstein's 2019 arrest, due to Epstein's personal connections, wealth, and influence."

The committee called on the Treasury Department and the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network to review whether banks and other financial institutions are meeting their obligations to report activity that could point to money laundering and human trafficking.

The committee has been investigating Epstein, Maxwell and their connections since last year.

#2945. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#2944. Falcons steal K Jake Moody from Ravens’ practice squad

The Baltimore Ravens need a new kicker. Again.

The Atlanta Falcons are signing Jake Moody off of the Ravens' practice squad, per NFL insider Jordan Schultz, robbing Baltimore of another kicker less than a week after Week 1 starter Tyler Loop landed on injured reserve with a groin injury.

The move raises questions about the Ravens' approach to their kicker situation with Loop sidelined for at least four games. The team had a roster spot available, but opted to elevate Moody from the practice squad to face the Jacksonville Jaguars last Thursday. With only two elevations remaining, he would have required a promotion to the 53-man roster to see out Loop's absence, which would have also precluded his departure to Atlanta. Logic suggests that the Ravens should have just promoted Moody knowing they would need him to kick for the rest of the month, maybe longer.

Additional reporting from Jeff Zrebiec of The Athletic indicated that the Ravens were "willing" to promote Moody to their active roster on Monday. The Falcons, though, offered a better situation after releasing kicker Jason Sanders: a potential long-term job for Moody and a home field enclosed by a dome.

It still seems, though, that Baltimore could have headed off this entire issue had they promoted Moody to the active roster last week. It is possible that his agent was keeping tabs on the Falcons' kicking situation and preferred a practice squad stay to preserve his options. However, Loop has not exactly inspired much confidence that he can deliver key kicks for a team that will need to win at the margins to make the playoffs this year. There is an argument that the Ravens should have identified Moody as their kicker for the foreseeable future and adjusted their roster accordingly.

Instead, they will have to scour the NFL's practice squads and free-agent pool to find their next kicker. Signing Nick Folk, who the Falcons cut to add Moody, would be an ironic twist. After three straight years of leading the league with a field goal conversion rate of 95.5% or better, the 42-year-old's numbers have collapsed in 2026. He made just 72.2% of his field goals to start the season with two misses inside of 50 yards and a one-for-four mark from beyond.

#2943. Anthropic sets IPO price range of $70 to $78, looks to start trading Nov. 19, sources say

Anthropic said it will offer 825 million shares in its upcoming IPO at a price range between $70 and $78 per share, according to an updated filing on Tuesday, bringing the 5-year-old artificial intelligence lab a step closer to a potentially historic Nasdaq debut.

The company is planning to price on Nov. 18 and trade on Nov. 19, according to people familiar with the details who were granted anonymity because they were not authorized to speak publicly. Anthropic is targeting retail allocation of about 20%, the people said, a much higher percentage than the typical initial public offering, which is 5% to 10% for retail investors.

At the high end of the range, Anthropic would net up to $64.35 billion in proceeds through the IPO. The company could end up being worth up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis, rising from the $965 billion valuation it announced as part of a funding round in May.

Anthropic said it has entered into an agreement with Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. to purchase $26 billion of the company's Class N common stock in a private placement at a price per share equal to the IPO price. The shares sold in the private placement will not be registered in the offering and will not be subject to lock-up agreements with the underwriters, the company said.

As Anthropic executives get set to pitch the offering to investors, they have to contend with a tumultuous political environment, following last week's midterm elections that included a Democratic sweep in Congress and the passage of numerous statewide ballot measures that could slow the AI buildout. They're also facing a public market that's grown increasingly skeptical of the AI trade on concern that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for companies that need it for costly infrastructure purchases.

CNBC reported last week that some Anthropic investors were bracing for a lower valuation than had previously been expected. While Anthropic had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation until recently, shareholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

Should Anthropic reach the Nasdaq this month as planned, under the ticker symbol "ANT," the debut would come roughly five months after SpaceX went public in a record offering that raised $85.7 billion. Elon Musk's reusable rocket and AI company has a market cap of just under $2 trillion, around its valuation at the time of the IPO.

Institutional demand for Anthropic is even stronger than it was for SpaceX at this point in the process, the people said. The company's book is already covered at the $70 to $78 per share range on an allocable basis, they said.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the AI lab in 2021 after he and a small group of researchers defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and the company primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

On Monday, Anthropic introduced two new AI models, Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2, the first time the company has released so-called frontier models since Amodei called to slow down the pace of AI development in September. Anthropic said that the models went through rigorous safety and alignment testing, and that the company developed new evaluations and post-launch monitoring tools ahead of the release.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic said in its amended filing Tuesday that its run-rate revenue was roughly $81 billion at the end of October, up $10 billion from the previous month.

"Our aim is to help humankind chart a safe course through this technological evolution, open new frontiers of discovery, and bring forth an age of miracles," Anthropic's co-founders wrote in the prospectus. "Everyone who owns our stock, whether an institution or an individual, becomes part of that effort. We hope you will share our ambition to see it though."

#2939. Alphabet shares appear to have bottomed. How to play it for a quick rebound

Ten days after Alphabet shares experienced their worst one-day drubbing in its history, the search, media and AI giant has barely retraced a faction of its nearly $500 billion in lost market capitalization, having rallied just a paltry 1.5%. The muted recovery stands out for a company that posted its 13th straight quarter of double-digit revenue growth in the same report, with Google Cloud revenue more than doubling. Alphabet's earnings were the first major crack to appear in the AI trade and foreshadowed a series of volatile events for the complex, including the election and the growing scandal enveloping Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang.

Still, a bullish case for Alphabet rests not in its post-earnings concerns being unfounded, but rather on its growth being strong enough to withstand them. On Monday, Google launched two new systems built on its custom AI chip, the TPU, a sign the company keeps evolving, and the debate over AI and search is far from settled. The sheer size of the drop, a record one-day loss after a quarter of 25% revenue growth, may also leave room for a reversion toward pre-earnings levels. For investors who agree, options offer a variety of ways to express that view.

Based on Monday's close, I'm buying the December 18 285/300 call spread for a debit of roughly $7.07.

Alphabet's 30-day implied volatility closed Monday around 35%, higher than about 44% of days over the past year. That is mid-range, so either buying or selling premium can be viable for this trade. A bull call spread is the higher-conviction route. It needs the stock to rally to pay off, but it offers a larger payout if the view is right. Selling a put spread is the lower-conviction route. It can carry a higher probability of profit, but with a smaller reward relative to the risk.

Due to the sheer size of the drop and the weak recovery thus far, I'm choosing the higher-conviction call spread.

Alphabet has recovered only a small part of its post-earnings loss, still down 11.3% from its pre-earnings close of $324.89. The trade expires Dec. 18 and assumes that gap at least partly closes at some point over the next 39 days, not necessarily right away.

The long 285 call sits just in the money, and the short 300 call sits about halfway back from the post-earnings low to the pre-earnings close. This gives the trade a breakeven of $292.07, 1.4% above Monday's close and still about 10% below pre-earnings levels.

The trade has a theoretical probability of profit of 47% and reaches its maximum profit of $793 per spread if Alphabet closes at or above $300 on Dec. 18. The maximum loss of $707 comes if shares close at or below $285. If Alphabet closes between $285 and $300 on Dec. 18, the long call can be exercised, leaving investors holding 100 shares per spread. Investors can close the spread before expiration to capture profits, cut losses, or avoid taking on shares.

This is a directional bet on a continued rebound, and the main risk is that the recovery stalls, even for a historically strong company. Nvidia's results on Nov. 17 could set the tone for the AI trade, and rates, energy prices, and scrutiny of data center construction remain headwinds to watch.

#2938. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2933. Amazon's Ring unveils flying drone and robot to patrol homes and businesses

Amazon's Ring is going deeper into surveillance with an indoor drone for home monitoring and an autonomous robot designed to patrol businesses.

The company on Tuesday unveiled the $250 Ring Drone Cam, a security camera that can fly from room to room, including upstairs, as well as capture footage during the day or at night. When an alarm or sensor on windows, doors and home security devices is triggered, the textbook-sized drone travels a preset path to investigate suspicious activity. Users can also deploy the drone at any time, the company said.

At a demo in New York on Monday, Ring CEO Jamie Siminoff said the drone could be used as a home monitoring tool and as a crime "deterrent" due to the device's built-in LED lights.

"If someone is going around your house at night, and your motion detector goes off on your Ring doorbell, imagine if you see spotlights and something flying across the window," Siminoff said. "You're probably not going to go into that house."

The drone, which can fly up to eight minutes at a time before autonomously returning to its charging dock, has two plastic-encased propellers that emit a loud buzz when it's airborne.

Ring devices have provided Amazon another avenue to grow its presence in the smart home beyond its Echo smart speakers and Eero routers, expanding in a market that Grand View Research estimates will top $200 billion in sales this year. It's equally fertile ground for Amazon rivals Apple and Google.

Ring first previewed its drone, then called the Always Home Cam, in 2020. Siminoff said he chose to hold off on releasing the device until Amazon could perfect the design, sensors and data processing.

The company on Tuesday also debuted the $1,000 Ring Rover Cam. The device repurposes Amazon's Astro home robot, which had a short life as a business device and has seen few upgrades since its 2021 debut, for companies to use to patrol offices, warehouses and other workspaces.

Ring said it "leveraged the best of Astro" to build the Ring Rover Cam, which it describes as a "purpose-built security robot."

Similar to the Astro, the device is a display mounted on a two-wheeled base with an extendable periscope camera. It also features a "built-in siren," two-way audio and a "light tower" that shines onto the ceiling to help users keep track of its whereabouts.

Siminoff said in an interview that the drone and the robot allow users to monitor multiple areas of their home or business with just one device.

"People like indoor cameras, but they don't necessarily want them everywhere around their home," Siminoff said.

Siminoff said the drone can't fly outside per Federal Aviation Administration regulations, which restrict operating drones unless they're within a line of sight of a pilot.

"We have told our customers not to do that because it's illegal," Siminoff said. "We've also put guardrails in there to make it so it doesn't fly outside."

The Ring Drone Cam ships in the U.S. on Jan. 27, pending approval from the Federal Communications Commission, while the Ring Rover Cam will begin shipping to users on Dec. 16, the company said.

#2927. Credit card debt climbs to a record $1.29 trillion, New York Fed research finds

Americans are increasingly turning to credit cards as financial pressures mount, new reports show.

Collectively, credit card balances climbed by $22 billion in the third quarter to a record $1.29 trillion, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of New York's latest household debt report, released Tuesday. Balances are up 4.2% from a year earlier.

Total household debt, including mortgages, auto loans and student loans, also expanded by $334 billion for the July-to-September period to a record $19.1 trillion.

"Overall debt has been growing," the New York Fed researchers said on a press call Tuesday. "This does reflect financial hardship out there."

The average credit card balance per consumer now stands at $6,678, up 2.4% year over year, a separate quarterly credit industry insights report from TransUnion found. The number of people with credit cards rose 2% to 178 million from 174.8 million.

Consumers often turn to credit cards in part because they are more accessible than other types of loans — even though the average interest rate on a new credit card offer is about 24%, according to data from LendingTree. Credit card APRs vary depending on the type of card, the issuer and the borrower's creditworthiness.

"Credit cards continue to be one of the most expensive ways to borrow," said Charlie Wise, TransUnion's senior vice president of global research and consulting.

Over time, an increasing share of new credit cards has gone to borrowers with lower incomes and credit scores, and "those accounts are now showing higher levels of distress," the New York Fed researchers said in a corresponding blog post.

Credit card issuers loosened their lending standards during the Covid pandemic, and delinquency rates rose in the years that followed, the New York Fed researchers said on the press call.

"Macroeconomic headwinds — namely high inflation and rising borrowing costs — have squeezed consumers, with inflation outpacing wage growth for low-income brackets," according to the New York Fed.

Meanwhile, the cost of living continues to climb, with Americans paying more for essentials such as groceries and gas.

Stubborn inflation has now stretched on for years, putting many households in an affordability squeeze. Costs for goods and services rose more than expected again in October, the Bureau of Labor Statistics also reported Tuesday.

"Higher prices and higher rates have pushed more people into debt," said Ted Rossman, principal consumer finance analyst at Money Management International, a nonprofit credit counseling agency.

Balances have continued to swell in the wake of the pandemic, and even though the growth rate is now slowing, "from a household finance standpoint, a lot of damage has been done," Rossman said.

The reports come on the heels of the Democrats' strong midterm results, when many voters expressed their dissatisfaction with the U.S. economy, according to Kyle Moore, chief economist at the nonpartisan think tank The Century Foundation.

"The throughline connecting so many of Tuesday's outcomes was a recognition that the American economy is failing American families," Moore said, referring to Election Day.

#2924. Jensen Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy further complicates Nvidia’s China problem

Following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's admission on Sunday to an extramarital affair with alleged Chinese spy Alice Huang, the chipmaker's relationship to China is about to come under intense scrutiny in Washington, D.C.

Since October 2022, when the Biden administration announced sweeping controls to limit China's ability to obtain advanced U.S. chips, Nvidia has seen its business diminish in the world's second-largest economy. Months after an even stricter set of rules was implemented by the Trump administration, Huang said in October 2025, "We are 100% out of China."

The recent allegations against Huang, while throwing the chipmaker into crisis, have also brought attention to the Nvidia CEO's potential influence over U.S. policy on chip sales to China, where its business once flourished. Of particular interest is whether restricted Nvidia chips have reached Chinese companies through customers like Singapore-based Megaspeed, the company linked to Alice Huang.

The New York Times first reported that the Chinese businesswoman was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, according to people briefed on the intelligence circulated in a memo among the Trump administration in the spring. China has called the allegation a smear.

Jensen Huang has been closely aligned with President Donald Trump during his second administration, helping advise the White House on artificial intelligence safety regulations, while also pushing for looser restrictions on selling AI chips to China. In December, Trump said Nvidia would be allowed to ship its H200 chips to China, provided the U.S. got a 25% cut of sales. In March, Huang told reporters that Nvidia had "received purchase orders, and we're in the process of restarting our manufacturing."

Huang was a last-minute addition to the roster of tech executives who joined Trump on a trip to China in May, after the president was briefed on intelligence concerns, according to the Times.

"In 35 years, I haven't seen a chip CEO with this much direct influence on export policy," said Patrick Moorhead, CEO at Moor Insights & Strategy, in an email.

Since last week's midterm elections that included sweeping victories across the country for Democrats, the mood in Washington has changed. Three House committees have requested closed-door briefings with the Nvidia CEO about his relationship with Megaspeed.

Democratic lawmakers, who are about to take power in the House and Senate, have said publicly that Congress will investigate and seek answers. Trump said Monday that he was "amazed" by the allegations and that Huang told him he "had no idea that she was a spy." The FBI is now looking into the issue, Trump said at a White House briefing.

It's been a long four years for Nvidia in China.

Before the initial export controls in 2022, China made up nearly a quarter of Nvidia's revenue. Nvidia responded to the Biden-era restrictions by redesigning some of its products to comply with the new regulations.

"It was a turbulent month or so as the company went upside down to re-engineer" the products, Huang told CNBC in a 2023 interview. Still, he was confident the company could continue selling chips to China. "I think we're going to be just fine in the ability to serve the customers there," he said.

After the Biden administration tightened export restrictions again, Nvidia redesigned another AI graphics processing unit for China, the H20. Following reports that Chinese model developer DeepSeek was using restricted Nvidia chips, the Trump administration limited sales of H20. Nvidia revealed it would incur $5.5 billion in costs related to canceled orders for the redesigned chip.

However, despite saying in its forecasts that "we're assuming 0% for China," the company maintained business in the region. In the last fiscal year, Nvidia reported that about 9% of its revenue came from China, including Hong Kong, although that includes revenue from segments outside of restricted data center chips, such as gaming, auto and industrial. Nvidia's China revenue was 8% in the most recent quarter.

A report in May from short seller Culper Research alleged Nvidia's China exposure was much higher. The firm said that more than 20% of Nvidia's fiscal 2026 compute revenue was driven by China, supported by "illegal GPU diversion and Southeast Asian intermediaries," according to the report.

A loophole allows Chinese companies to rent Nvidia GPUs in data centers, even when they're restricted from buying them outright. For example, a Chinese company could lease Nvidia computing power in Europe by renting server capacity from a non-Chinese neocloud or data center operator that's exempt from export controls.

Nvidia didn't respond to a request for comment for this story.

Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer based in Singapore, has been accused of diverting its own supply of Nvidia's restricted AI chips to Chinese companies. Megaspeed denies wrongdoing and said inspections found no evidence of violations.

In 2025, Nvidia changed the way it reported regional sales to categorize by customer headquarters, instead of by where the products were billed. Nvidia also stopped reporting Singapore sales during the same period.

"Whether Blackwell chips reached China is a Megaspeed question that predates this week," said Moorhead, pointing to Bloomberg reporting in December 2025 that found Megaspeed had imported 50,000 more Nvidia chips than Nvidia tallied in its data centers.

In 2025, a House Select Committee opened an investigation into whether China's DeepSeek operates on Nvidia's chips.

A separate investigation by the U.S. Commerce Department was centered on Megaspeed. That probe was abruptly transferred to the Justice Department, the Times reported, following an email from Nvidia's general counsel to the Commerce Department seeking information about the investigation.

The restrictions aren't just coming from the U.S. China has continued to limit the amount of Nvidia chips it will allow into the country, keeping Nvidia's China sales numbers down. The U.S. has licensed H200 sales to Alibaba, Tencent, ByteDance and other Chinese companies, but Beijing hasn't approved large-scale imports, Moorhead told CNBC. Nvidia said H200 shipments to China made up less than 1% of data center revenue for the quarter that ended in July.

Moorhead expects more export control legislation to pass as a result of the latest turmoil. Proposals could allow Congress oversight of chip export licenses or require chipmakers to install security mechanisms to track the location of chips to determine if they've found their way to China.

"Every China license now gets read through this story," Moorhead said. "Jensen has been Nvidia's lead voice on China, and that voice is weaker."

— CNBC's Kif Leswing and Seema Mody contributed reporting to this report.

Correction: Three House committees have requested closed-door briefings with the Nvidia CEO about his relationship with Megaspeed. An earlier version misstated the time element.

#2922. Grading the trade: Josh Downs gives Chiefs WR help they needed, but is he the right fit?

Just prior to the trade deadline, the Kansas City Chiefs acquired wide receiver Josh Downs from the Indianapolis Colts. The Chiefs will receive Downs and a 2027 seventh-round pick, and the Colts will get back fourth- and fifth-round picks in 2027.

Downs, 25, who recently requested a trade, got his wish and now will team up with Patrick Mahomes. In nine games (seven starts) with the Colts this season, Downs caught 33 passes for 354 yards and one TD on 57 targets.

Downs also had a team-high seven catches for 72 yards on nine targets in the Colts' 33-30 loss in Kansas City back in Week 2.

Who won this trade? Let's dive into the debate.

The Chiefs were in big need of adding receiver help. Not one wideout on the roster has either 50 targets or 300 yards receiving on the season. Downs, sharing the lead role in Indy along with Keenan Allen (and with Alec Pierce hurt), has surpassed both marks.

That's not to say that he instantly will become Patrick Mahomes' go-to target. But it's hard not to imagine Downs stepping in and earning a fairly sizable role in Kansas City's offense, assuming he can develop a relationship with Mahomes and pick up the playbook quickly.

Downs has primarily been a slot receiver in his career, although his work rate playing outside has increased slightly in 2026 out of need. It would make sense to put him in the slot, although what makes him, Rashee Rice and Xavier Worthy attractive is that they can line up in multiple spots.

Rice's numbers over the past year-plus have been slightly better inside, but Worthy has been far more efficient as a perimeter receiver in his career. That likely means Downs can take those snaps and do what he does best: run efficient routes, separate quickly and generate first downs. The biggest drawback is that Downs is not a deep threat, which was probably their biggest need.

This move also can help the Chiefs' punt-return situation. Nikko Remigio has struggled in that role this season. Downs has only returned two punts this season, but he has a very respectable 9.8-yard career average on punt returns.

The Chiefs know that Downs is a free agent after the season, and they had to be comfortable with the idea of this trade potentially being a half-season rental. Thinning out their Day 3 pick allotment stings a little, but the price wasn't that steep in the big picture, especially when you compare it to what the Seahawks paid for Rashid Shaheed (fourth- and fifth-round picks) and what the Jaguars dealt for Jakobi Meyers (fourth- and sixth-rounders) before last year's deadline.

This move is about taking advantage of a championship window with Mahomes showing he's recovered from his knee injury and Travis Kelce still performing at a relatively high level in his expected swan-song season.

Downs requested a trade and got his wish. The Colts likely weren't convinced they had to extend him, and Downs probably wasn't returning to Indianapolis next season anyway, so trading him now made perfect sense instead of waiting for compensatory-pick returns more than a year from now.

It fits right in line with the other trades they've made since firing GM Chris Ballard, shipping out one-time franchise pillars Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner and also dealing Allen. When you consider what they got back for Downs, which was two early Day 3 picks, it was a pretty fair market price.

Not having a first-rounder next spring from the Sauce Gardner will continue to sting. But with the trades, they've managed to replenish the cupboard a bit. They already have seven Day 3 picks to work with and are projected to receive two more compensatory selections in that same range.

Having those picks can allow the Colts potentially to slide up in the draft, and quarterback could be on the menu given the uncertainty surrounding Daniel Jones, even if Jones was not traded by the deadline.

They'll also eventually need to replenish a gutted WR room, but for now the Colts can see what they have in the likes of Nick Westbrook-Ikhine, Anthony Gould, Ashton Dulin, Deion Burks, Laquon Treadwell and, of course, Pierce, when he's back healthy again. It wouldn't be stunning if the Colts only bring back a few of these players next year, but the remainder of the season will help them decide who stays and who goes.

#2919. Homeowners tap equity as HELOC balances post biggest jump since 2005: New York Fed report

Homeowners tapped their home equity lines of credit in the third quarter at the highest rate in more than two decades, according to a new Federal Reserve Bank of New York report on household debt released Tuesday.

Balances on HELOCs jumped by $23 billion during the three months ending Sept. 30, the largest increase since 2005. That brings the outstanding amount owed to $481 billion, the report shows. This also marks the 18th consecutive quarterly increase.

HELOC balances peaked at roughly $714 billion in the first quarter of 2009, according to a 2015 New York Fed report.

The current rise in balances comes amid a sluggish housing market, due at least partly to the so-called mortgage rate lock-in effect. Just under half of outstanding mortgages — 49.1% — carried rates of 4% or lower in the second quarter, according to a Realtor.com analysis of federal mortgage data, leaving many owners reluctant to give up their low rates to purchase another home at current higher borrowing costs.

The median price of an existing home was $417,500 in September, according to the National Association of Realtors, a real estate trade group. While up just 1.3% from a year earlier, the amount is nearly 54% higher than in September 2019, according to NAR. The average rate on a typical 30-year fixed-rate mortgage is 7.3% as of Tuesday, according to Mortgage News Daily.

"HELOCs let them borrow against their equity without giving up the low interest rate on their home," said certified financial planner Douglas Boneparth, president and founder of Bone Fide Wealth in New York City and a member of CNBC's Financial Advisor Council.

Mortgage holders collectively had $17.5 trillion in home equity as of the third quarter, according to a new report from Intercontinental Exchange, or ICE, a financial markets technology and data company.

Tappable equity that can be accessed — meaning a homeowner's equity would remain at or above 20% of the home's value after tapping it — rose 1.2%, or $16,600, from a year ago to an average of $204,000 per borrower, the ICE report shows.

"Homeowners are sitting on substantial housing wealth, with 2.4 million mortgage holders now holding at least $1 million in equity," Andy Walden, head of mortgage and Housing Market Research at ICE, said in the report.

HELOCs let you tap a line of credit over time as you need the money, instead of getting it all at once like a home equity loan or cash-out refinance.

While HELOCs may have fewer up-front costs than a home equity loan, they generally have a variable interest rate. This means it will move up and down based on a benchmark like the prime rate, which banks use as a basis to set rates on a variety of loans. And, while the Federal Reserve doesn't control that rate, it is influenced by changes the Fed makes to the so-called federal funds rate.

"If your budget only works at today's rate, then your budget doesn't really work," Boneparth said of HELOCs. "You kind of have to be mindful of the fact that rates may go up."

The average interest rate for a $30,000 HELOC was 7.49% as of Nov. 4, according to Bankrate.

Many HELOCs also have a "draw" period when you can take money out, which often lasts five or 10 years. During that time, you typically are only required to pay the interest on any funds you've withdrawn. After that, however, you'll enter a repayment period of, say, 10 or 20 years, when you're required to pay both interest and principal. Because of that, your payments will jump if you've only been paying interest.

"The real test comes when the repayment period kicks in, and the principal starts to show up, if that's how their HELOC is structured," Boneparth said. "Payments potentially could double overnight, and you really want to know that before you sign anything."

Additionally, consider what you're planning to use the money for, Boneparth said.

"If you're borrowing against your home to improve your home, that could make a lot of sense," Boneparth said. "Borrowing against your house to pay off credit cards is a little trickier because you're turning unsecured debt into debt secured by the roof over your head."

In that case, if your spending habits don't change, he said, "you're just moving the problem somewhere a little scarier."

#2915. How much you can make in 2027 and still pay 0% capital gains tax

The IRS has released the capital gains tax brackets for 2027, which include a six-figure earnings limit for the 0% rate for married couples.

If you're eager to sell profitable brokerage account investments, higher income thresholds for the 0% rate could help reduce your tax bill, experts say.

"The 0% capital gains bracket is easily one of the most overlooked strategies for tax-free income," said Stephen Maggard, a certified financial planner with Abacus Planning Group in Columbia, South Carolina.

But it's important to know how the tax brackets work and the possible trade-offs before selling investments.

Despite recent stock market volatility, many investors have portfolio gains after years of market growth. As of market close Nov. 9, the S&P 500 was up about 10% year-to-date. The index also returned more than 16% in 2025, and over 23% in 2024.

If you're planning to leverage the 0% capital gains bracket in 2027 to sell or rebalance brokerage account assets, here are some key things to know.

The capital gains brackets — with rates of 0%, 15% or 20% — apply to assets owned for more than one year, known as long-term capital gains. By contrast, investments held for one year or less are short-term gains, subject to regular income tax rates.

Your rate depends on taxable income, which you calculate by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income, or AGI. Taxable income also includes any gains from investments that year.

The standard deduction will rise to $16,600 for single taxpayers and $33,200 for married couples in 2027.

For example, let's say a married couple's AGI is $130,000 for 2027, including capital gains. After subtracting the $33,200 standard deduction, their taxable income would be $96,800, which falls below the 0% threshold.

However, taxable income should include mutual fund distributions, which can "dramatically alter year-end planning projections" and increase taxable income, according to CFP Jim Guarino, managing director at Baker Newman Noyes in Woburn, Massachusetts. He is also a certified public accountant.

If your federal taxable income is above the 0% threshold, you'll pay 15% on the portion of investment gains that exceed the 0% limit, up to that bracket's cap. Gains above that may be taxed at 20%.

Also, the 0% capital gains bracket applies to federal taxes, but your profits could still be subject to state-level taxes, experts say.

As income limits rise for the 0% capital gains bracket due to IRS inflation adjustments, more investors could have tax savings opportunities, experts say.

"There is tons of planning to be done here," especially for early retirees with lower income, according to CFP Dinon Hughes, a partner with Nvest Financial in Portsmouth, New Hampshire.

One strategy, known as "tax gain harvesting," is selling profitable brokerage account assets during lower-income years to leverage the 0% bracket, he said. Keep in mind, these sales stack on top of your other earnings, which could bump taxable income above the 0% threshold.

You can also sell the asset and immediately rebuy it to "reset the cost basis," or original purchase price, said CFP Charles Sachs, chief investment officer of Imperio Wealth Advisors in Coral Gables, Florida.

By resetting the cost basis to current market value, you can reduce future capital gains to trim your tax bill.

When selling assets at a loss, the "wash sale rule" blocks tax-loss harvesting when you rebuy a "substantially identical" asset within 30 days before or after the sale. But the same rule doesn't apply when harvesting gains.

Managing capital gains is only one part of yearly tax planning, according to Guarino with Baker Newman Noyes.

Before selling investments, you should know how boosting AGI could impact your broader financial plan, including income phase-outs for various tax breaks, he said.

For example, modified adjusted gross income can impact a range of tax benefits, such as Affordable Care Act health insurance premium subsidies, which can disappear once earnings exceed the federal limits by even a single dollar.

Another example is President Donald Trump's temporary $6,000 senior "bonus" deduction, which is worth up to $12,000 for married couples per year through 2028.

#2912. Poll: Do you find the Brown-Tatum drama narrative interesting?

Tonight marks the first regular season game between the Philadelphia 76ers and the Boston Celtics since the offseason trade sending Jaylen Brown to Philadelphia took place. Understandably, we're receiving a fresh wave of reports and rehashing of events surrounding the purported fallout between Brown and Celtics star Jayson Tatum. I feel compelled to ask, do you find this narrative interesting?

I'll start here: I totally understand why this is a thing. The NBA has become a 24-hour, 365-day entertainment vehicle that many of us consume obsessively. That reality is driven as much by drama between personalities, both real and manufactured, as by what actually takes place on the court. Brown and Tatum played together for nine seasons, their entire careers overlapping for all but one year, for a club perennially in contention which won one NBA title. If there was a fallout between them, and there seems like there's definitely at least something there, that's a story. If Brown had a disagreement with how he was treated by Celtics management, that's a topic worth exploring. We've hit on those points on this very website (thanks for reading, tell your friends!). If you have lamb chops baking in the oven today and popcorn ready for your viewing experience tonight, cool. I get it.

Still, I can't find myself really caring about it. Whatever happened, it all happened behind the scenes. There was no public infighting or drawn-out trade demand. The Jaylen Brown trade came completely out of left field like a flash of lightning. What Brown said the other day almost exactly aligns with my thinking on the matter:

In today's NBA, guys change teams all the time. Plenty of breakups have been a lot messier than this one, including in Philadelphia. I'll chuckle when someone makes a clever joke about everything, but beyond that, I can't find myself getting invested in the whole thing. It's Philadelphia vs. Boston. That's more than enough for me.

How about you? Are you still finding this drama interesting or is it entirely in the rear view mirror for you as well? Vote and let us know how you feel in the comments.

#2911. U.S. gas prices fall below $4 for the first time since July

Americans may finally find some relief at the pump.

The national average price of regular gasoline fell below $4 a gallon for the first time since July, reaching $3.9788 on Tuesday, according to AAA. Prices are down about 17 cents from a week ago and nearly 39 cents from a month ago.

The decline comes after energy inflation slowed significantly in October. Energy prices increased 0.7% during the month, a far cry from the 5.2% surge seen in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. The BLS' gasoline index increased 1% over the month, after advancing 9.1% in September.

Bespoke Investment Group pointed out that gasoline prices also tend to decline at this time of the year, making part of the recent relief seasonal.

"While lower oil prices have been a tailwind for the relief at the pump, it's also perfectly normal for gas prices to be falling at this time of year, so if they weren't, there would really be something wrong," the firm said.

The drop at the pump followed a sharp retreat in crude oil. International oil benchmark Brent crude on Tuesday briefly fell below $90 a barrel for the first time since August as comments from U.S. President Donald Trump fueled hopes for an end to Washington's nearly nine-month conflict with Iran.

Recovering oil flows from the Persian Gulf have also eased supply concerns. Regional crude exports have returned to an estimated 80% to 85% of prewar levels, including pipeline shipments, according to ING.

"Energy markets remain under pressure as hopes for a de-escalation in the Middle East reduce geopolitical risk premiums. Oil extended its losses as Gulf supply flows continued to recover, while European gas also moved lower," analysts at ING wrote.

However, the Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller warned Tuesday that energy shocks may have more persistent effects on inflation than they did before the pandemic.

"While I am pleased to see energy prices down from their high rates over the past week or so, they are still quite elevated relative to the start of the year, and the ultimate effect on inflation will depend on what levels are sustained," he said.

#2909. Fernando Mendoza gets 3-word message from Kirk Cousins after Raiders trade

Fernandomania has officially begun in the desert.

With the Las Vegas Raiders trading quarterback Kirk Cousins to the Minnesota Vikings before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline, Las Vegas is expected to turn to Fernando Mendoza, the No. 1 pick in the draft earlier this year, under center.

Mendoza is slated to make his first career start against the defending Super Bowl champion Seattle Seahawks at home on Sunday, Nov. 15 (4:05 ET).

Mendoza's lone regular-season action came during his debut, at the end of a Week 4 road loss to the New England Patriots on Oct. 11. He finished 6-for-8 passing for 48 yards. General manager John Spytek confirmed that Mendoza will be the team's starter moving forward during a news conference Nov. 10.

"He passed every test," Spytek said. "I want to stop short of saying he's earned it, but he put us in a position where we had to make a really hard decision today, with his growth as a person, as a teammate, as a leader, as a quarterback."

Spytek said he never made a call shopping Cousins, and that talks with the Vikings started in earnest Tuesday morning after the Vikings' 23-6 drubbing on "Monday Night Football" at the hands of the Buffalo Bills. The organization was clear with Cousins upon signing him to what is basically a one-year deal in March that Mendoza is obviously the future of the franchise.

Cousins and Spytek met before the trade was agreed to. While it wasn't an easy conversation, Spytek said, it was productive and the Raiders felt like it was important they showed him respect as a 15-year veteran.

"We're all kind of staring the future in the face here with Fernando," said Spytek, in his second year as GM of the "Silver and Black."

Spytek added: "We had to basically decide do we want to go forward here over the last eight games, or do we want to take what Minnesota is offering right now? Ultimately, we decided to do that."

Mendoza's first two opponents boast vaunted defensive units. Seattle's defense, dubbed the "Dark Side" for a reason, did not allow a touchdown last week to the undermanned Arizona Cardinals. Looking ahead, the Raiders face the Denver Broncos on the road in Week 11 then the Cleveland Browns. Denver ranks in the top 10 in points allowed per game (20.7), sacks (26) and takeaways (12).

"Fernando has everything we've asked him to do in practice," head coach Klint Kubiak said. "I think he's exceeded those expectations, and we all know that live bullet Sunday is far different from practice. However, he showed us that he's ready, not only physically but mentally. He's up there studying right now like he would any other game. He puts a lot of work in. His teammates know how prepared he is. It's not just lip service, but he has shown it on the practice field."

Kubiak rattled off the number of ways Cousins influenced Mendoza, the reigning Heisman Trophy winner. The examples he offered included working through a progression, recognizing blitzes, taking care of the body and ways to connect with teammates, Kubiak said, "there's a lot more to playing the position than what your coaches tell you in the meeting room."

"I think Kirk embodied all the right things there, how to prepare and how to be a professional, and really, when things aren't going well, what buttons to push on your teammates and what questions to ask your coaches," Kubiak said. "It's an endless list of all positive things."

On his Instagram story, Cousins offered a goodbye to "Raider Nation" and a message to Mendoza, whom Cousins tagged in the post.

"I feel that Fernando has done a great job," Cousins told reporters Oct. 26. "He has a bright future ahead. I'm going to try and go out there and play as well as I possibly can and try and win and give him a good example to learn from."

Toughening matters for Mendoza on the field will be the lack of a rushing attack for the Raiders. First-round talent Ashton Jeanty from the 2025 draft has not worked out, although rookie Mike Washington has shown some juice in the backfield. Still, Las Vegas ranks 31st in rushing yards per game (71.3) and is last in yards per rush (2.9).

Similar to Cousins, Mendoza will likely have to shoulder much of the output in the Raiders' offense.

"The game has to be balanced," Kubiak said following the team's most recent loss to the San Francisco 49ers that dropped their record to 4-5. "You can't be a running and throwing team when you're throwing it every time. That's not easy.

"You got to start the game a lot faster. You got to go toe-to-toe in the first half so that the second half isn't a throwing fest."

The good news is that tight end Brock Bowers ranks ninth in the league in receiving yards and provides a crucial red-zone target with six touchdowns already this season. Wide receiver Tre Tucker emerged as a reliable downfield option for Cousins, which gives Mendoza more relief in the passing game.

#2907. Traders bet on a pop for Anthropic's historic IPO

News of Anthropic's historic initial public offering provided a wave of relief for tech investors. The response in the perpetual futures market might provide even more relief.

As Anthropic prepares to raise money in its initial public offering this month, traders in offshore markets are making large bets that there will be a big pop on the AI giant's debut.

Trying to guess an initial first day IPO reaction is nearly impossible, but a new and fast-growing corner of the markets might offer some clues.

Pre-IPO perpetuals, contracts that aim to track the price of private companies, trade on decentralized crypto exchanges like Hyperliquid, Coinbase, Binance and OKX to name a few. The current Hyperliquid Anthropic contract implies a stock price of $98 and its market cap at roughly $1.7 trillion, more than 30% higher than the $70-$78 range announced in the company's updated S1.

While trading on the exchange is not currently available to American investors, about $250 million of volume traded across Hyperliquid's two Anthropic trackers in the past 24 hours, according to the exchange.Hyperliquid's pre-IPO Anthropic perp began trading two weeks ago at $138 before settling into a roughly ten-dollar range between $110 and $120 prior to last week's selloff in tech stocks, as the contract slipped below $94 at one point.To proponents of this new asset class, the pricing of the perps is evidence that bankers intentionally undersell equity ahead of IPOs to ensure a post-market "pop." Looking at SpaceX, the argument rings true: pre-IPO perps on SpaceX traded at a premium and converged with equity prices at the IPO, all before shares went on a three-day rally of as much as 40% in the immediate aftermath of the listing.News of Anthropic's upcoming roadshow may be weighing on competitor OpenAI. Perps tracking OpenAI's market cap dropped 7% to $1.27 trillion, according to Hyperliquid trading on a contract trading $2.8 billion in volume over the past 24 hours.Shares of Hyperliquid Strategies (PURR), the publicly-traded Treasury company investing in the HYPE tokens that power the exchange fell 4% Tuesday. The stock is up 150% on the year.
