#2904. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2903. NBA analyst suggests Warriors use Kristaps Porziņģis to trade for Magic center

NBA analyst suggests Warriors use Kristaps Porziņģis to trade for Magic center originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

The Warriors currently are on a historic win streak, but one NBA analyst believes they need to make a move to shore up their interior.

On Monday's episode of "The Kevin O'Connor Show," Yahoo Sports' Kevin O'Connor told guest Nekias Duncan why Golden State should trade for Orlando Magic center Wendell Carter Jr.

"The Warriors should trade Kristaps Porziņģis with as many picks as it takes for Trey Murphy or Wendell Carter Jr.," O'Connor said. "To me, I think Wendell Carter would make a lot of sense if the Magic continue sliding here, and we'll see if they do.

"The Magic are in the East; they've stunk so far. Maybe they'll turn things around at some point, but Carter, to me — his size, 270 pounds, the rebounding that he provides, the connective passing and playmaking that I think is a bit underutilized in his career with the Magic. I've always been a fan of his passing vision out of the elbow areas. I think it would fit that Warriors offense.

"He can shoot the ball. He's shooting the ball better than ever so far this year at over 40 percent, and we'll see if he sustains that. But ultimately, I think Carter is a really good player who's been stuck in some not-so-good situations so far in his career in Chicago and Orlando, and I think for Golden State that would be my top target."

Carter Jr. is averaging 14.8 points and 9.6 rebounds per game on 63-percent shooting from the field and 47.8-percent shooting from the 3-point line, taking 2.6 attempts per game.

At 6-foot-10 and 270 pounds, Carter Jr. would instantly become the Warriors' biggest player. Center Charles Bassey also is listed at 6-foot-10 but lacks Carter Jr.'s size, weighing just 230 pounds.

Kerr has played Bassey next to veteran forward Draymond Green for just 10 minutes this season, despite Golden State's plus-16.7 net rating in that small sample size.

Carter Jr.'s presence at the rim would allow Green to roam on defense as a "middle linebacker," and his ability to stretch the floor could further improve the spacing of the NBA's top shooting team.

With star Jimmy Butler looking to return around the NBA All-Star break, O'Connor believes that the Warriors' top priority should be bringing in a big man.

"It should be center over any other position — you want to have a big who can play next to Draymond," O'Connor said. "You want to be able to space or play in the interior, and Carter can provide you a little bit of both in ways that old man (Al) Horford can't. The way, obviously, Porziņģis can't even get on the floor, but the idea of it is a guy who can either space or play inside. So I'd be going after Wendell Carter if the Magic continue sliding."

The Magic sit at 2-7, 12th in the Eastern Conference, and are without forward Paolo Banchero, the No. 1 overall draft pick in the 2022 NBA Draft, because of an ankle injury.

As Golden State works to move Porziņģis, Carter Jr. could be an avenue that the team pursues.

#2898. Las Vegas Raiders get their gold mined by the 49ers

The Las Vegas Raiders traveled to Levi's Stadium in Santa Clara, California, to renew the Battle of the Bay rivalry with the San Francisco 49ers. A rivalry that has meant so much to the two fan bases. Turnovers were the story of this contest. When the clock read zero, the Raiders had committed five turnovers and allowed 30+ points on defense for the sixth straight week.

Kirk Cousins had his worst game as the signal caller for the Raiders. In the first half, he only threw the ball eight times. By the end, Cousins threw three interceptions, with one pick-six after recovering his own fumble. Things simply did not go his way today.

Ashton Jeanty regained his starting position and managed 25 yards on six carries. In the passing game where he has made his largest impact, he caught five passes for -5.0 yards. Mike Washington Jr. was subjected to simply awful run blocking, finishing the game with -3.0 yards on six carries with a long of four yards.

Bowers played his first game near his hometown of Napa, California, with reportedly around 50 friends and family in attendance. He would punish the 49ers, finishing with 111 yards on seven catches, including a 21-yard score. A questionable spot robbed him of a second touchdown.

Early on, Raiders defensive playcaller Rob Leonard continued getting his lunch money stolen by an opposing offensive play caller. The 49ers drove down the field on the opening possession in 12 plays, five passes, seven runs and scored. On the second possession, the 49ers ran the ball down the Raiders' throats. Brock Purdy, the 49ers quarterback, would finish the game with 40 yards rushing on seven carries. Including a rushing touchdown which put the 49ers up 14-0.

By the end of the 49ers third drive the Raiders defense surrendered 94 yards rushing, while being on the field for every play except three with 1:52 seconds to play in the first quarter. What we heard from the television broadcasters was that it was 49ers head coach Kyle Shanahan's goal to run the ball 40 times against the Raiders' putrid run defense.

After going three-and-out on their first drive, Kirk Cousins gets settled in and hits passes to Jack Bech, Brock Bowers, and Jalen Nailor. Driving the Raiders downfield when the end of the quarter makes them switch sides. Cousins finishes the drive off with his team leading with the second rushing touchdown of the year. Drawing the Raiders to within a score.

Raiders' defense continued to allow nearly six yards per carry to the 49ers offense. On 4th and 1, the run blocking of the 49ers knocked the Raiders' defenders three yards off the line of scrimmage. Raiders followed it up by running some type of zone blitz where both defensive tackles dropped into zone coverage and surrendered a passing touchdown to McCaffrey.

After a Raiders offensive possession netting a field goal, the Raiders were down 21-10 and needed a defensive stop to close out the half. Maxx Crosby would get his fourth sack of the season and a false start penalty would end the half. At the end of the half, the 49ers had ran the ball 25 times for 125 yards and two touchdowns.

First play of the second half, Cousins fumbled the football, picked it up and threw a pick-6, 17 seconds into the second half. Exactly what the Raiders could not afford to have happen. To be fair, everything is resting on his shoulders and in order for the Raiders to have any chance of winning any game, he can't afford to make a mistake.

In the retribution drive after the turnover, Cousins came right back, completing seven of seven throws. finishing with a 21-yard touchdown pass to Bowers to level the score at 28-17. Mistakes happen, but atoning for them is critical. With just under 10 minutes remaining in the 3rd quarter, the 49ers offense would take the field for the first time in the second half.

Treydan Stukes, the Raiders' second-round pick, fooled Purdy, intercepted the pass, tried to fall numerous times with no one around to tackle him and stumbled back to the 30-yard line of the 49ers. Momentum shifts are a huge thing, giving the Raiders an opportunity to get it back to a one-score game. However, in the most self-sabotaging way ever, Cousins nearly threw an interception to a defensive lineman; the very next play, Fred Warner peanut-punches the ball out of Bowers hands and the 49ers recover the ball.

Four plays later, the Raiders get a 4th-and-short stop and a turnover on downs for the first time in a long time. Five turnovers were committed by both teams in the third quarter alone.

On the next drive, Cousins hits Michael Mayer for a nice gain and as he fought to reach the goal line, disaster struck. Mayer was tackled and fell on top of a defender, while another 49ers defender punched the ball out of his hands just before he crossed the goal line. Negating a potential score, turning the ball over yet again, giving the 49ers a touchback.

From there, the 49ers would drive past midfield with a few runs. But the lack of a Raiders pass rush gave Purdy enough time to find Jordan Watkins open for a 37-yard touchdown catch.

On the ensuing possession, the Raiders drove to the goal line again, behind a 33-yard catch-and-run from Bowers that appeared to stretch the ball over the pylon but was ruled out of bounds. Cousins' quarterback sneak was stuffed when Warner leaped over the pile after the snap. On the next play, Mayer was called for offensive pass interference, negating his own touchdown catch. Followed up with Cousins' second interception of the game, the second turnover inside the red zone, turnover number four and the third straight turnover.

But the hits just kept on coming; in junk time, Cousins would throw his third interception of the day, fourth straight turnover and fifth total. Cousins had Tre Tucker open deep, loaded up, putting his full body into the throw, only for it to fall short and into the waiting arms of a 49ers defender, mercifully allowing this game to come to an end.

#2897. Op-ed: In Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's alleged China spy scandal, there's plenty of blame to go around

To learn more about the CNBC CFO Council, visit cnbccouncils.com/cfo

In June 2013, President Barack Obama welcomed Chinese President Xi Jinping to Sunnylands, a secluded California estate, for an informal summit. Behind the diplomatic pleasantries, Obama confronted Xi over Chinese state-directed cyber-enabled espionage and theft of American intellectual property, warning that such activities threatened American companies and the broader bilateral relationship.

Thirteen years after the Sunnylands Summit, the threat has evolved significantly as the race to dominate AI frames the U.S.-China relationship. The stakes are higher, the technology more consequential, and the vulnerabilities increasingly involve attempts to compromise or coopt the "key man" at the helm of the nation's top technology companies.

With the unfolding scandal involving Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the blame should start with the CEO himself, but it should not end there. The situation illustrates a failure of Silicon Valley, corporate and political leadership at a much wider level.

Huang has acknowledged an extramarital affair with a woman identified in reporting as Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer. American intelligence officials reportedly believe she was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information and potentially influence Nvidia's efforts to loosen American semiconductor export restrictions. China denies the allegations.

Nvidia's independent directors have launched an investigation, and so far, they say no compromise of company secrets or national security information has been established. But the problem extends well beyond Huang or Nvidia. Focusing exclusively on whether proprietary technology or trade secrets were transferred fundamentally misunderstands the potential intelligence value of such an operation.

For Beijing, gaining insight into, influencing, or potentially compromising American technology policymaking could be equally valuable. Advance knowledge of export-control deliberations, regulatory intentions, negotiating positions, or divisions within the administration could provide strategic advantages without a single semiconductor design being stolen.

The Trump administration reportedly received warnings about the relationship before the scandal became public. Nevertheless, Huang remained an influential participant in semiconductor policy discussions while advocating greater access to China's market.

New reporting also raises questions about interactions between Nvidia and Commerce Department officials during an investigation into Megaspeed, subsequently transferred to the Justice Department. No improper influence has been established, but corporate access to government decision-making deserves scrutiny alongside proprietary technology.

Whether appropriate protective measures were taken remains unclear. President Donald Trump's public defense of Huang — saying that Huang told him he had no idea the woman could be a spy and that he trusted the CEO, and that if national intelligence had warned the president about this threat, as has been reported, he didn't remember — hardly conveyed the urgency warranted by allegations of a foreign intelligence operation targeting a strategically indispensable American company.

Trump has met Xi three times since returning to office, with two additional encounters anticipated in November and December. These meetings offer Trump an opportunity to follow Obama's example by confronting Xi directly over Chinese state-directed or sponsored espionage targeting American technology companies, their executives, and potentially the U.S. policymaking process.

It is also time for a more formal check from Congress on Silicon Valley, which has long celebrated the visionary founder, the indispensable executive whose genius supposedly transcends conventional corporate management and traditional corporate governance. Investors have rewarded this concentration of authority, while boards have all too frequently accommodated it.

Corporate governance professionals call this "Key Man" risk: excessive organizational dependence on an individual whose departure, financial misconduct, or moral compromise could materially damage the enterprise. In the artificial intelligence era, this risk becomes considerably more dangerous. When a handful of executives control technologies central to America's economic competitiveness, military capabilities, and intelligence advantages, their personal vulnerabilities become potential national security vulnerabilities.

When those executives also enjoy extraordinary access to the White House and senior policymakers, they become potential conduits for foreign intelligence collection and influence directed not simply at their companies, but at the American people and American policy itself.

Congress has opportunities to strengthen America's defenses and must act immediately.

The AI OVERWATCH Act would strengthen oversight of advanced semiconductor exports and associated national security licensing conditions.

The MATCH Act would address weaknesses in restrictions on advanced semiconductor manufacturing equipment, including foreign suppliers and servicing arrangements.

The Chip Security Act would strengthen mechanisms for tracking advanced chips and detecting diversion.

Importantly, the Remote Access Security Act would extend export-control authorities to certain remote access arrangements, including cloud computing, addressing the possibility that restricted American technological capabilities could be accessed without physically exporting the underlying equipment.

Together, these measures address important technological and regulatory gaps and vulnerabilities. But controlling chips, equipment, and computing access admittedly will not solve the human vulnerabilities problem — the so-called "Key Man" risks to U.S. technology supremacy and national security.

Most of these bills have a good amount of Congressional bipartisan support. However, they have encountered stiff resistance from the technology industry and their lobbyists, and some very detailed disagreements over specifics of export-control policy. There has also been uncertainty about White House support for these bills and an unwillingness by the Republican-controlled Congress to get on the wrong side of the White House. However, there is no doubt in my mind the Nvidia controversy has given supporters a new argument for action. The Democratic takeover of both chambers could further strengthen congressional oversight and an appetite for codifying much of this into law using these pieces of legislation.

There should also be a serious discussion among key U.S. government and corporate governance experts about whether strategically important technology companies need explicit board-level counterintelligence responsibilities, independent executive-security assessments, and confidential mechanisms for reporting foreign intelligence approaches to the appropriate channels.

This is not about morally policing executives' private lives. It is about recognizing that individuals controlling America's most sensitive technologies, and influencing the policies governing them, are valuable intelligence targets and assets if compromised.

Unfortunately, the Trump administration's broader philosophy appears to emphasize voluntary industry commitments and confidence in technology executives' ability to police themselves. Morality is not a governance system. Personal assurances are not counterintelligence safeguards. Importantly, shareholder interests are not always synonymous with the national interest.

Obama understood at Sunnylands that protecting American innovation required confronting China directly. That engagement helped to clearly articulate American redlines against cyber-enabled theft of intellectual property for commercial advantage.

Trump now has an opportunity, and a responsibility, to extend that principle to today's intelligence threats. At his forthcoming meetings with Xi, he should make clear that state-directed or sponsored operations targeting American technology executives and attempts to compromise U.S. technology policymaking are unacceptable and carry consequences for the bilateral relationship.

But confronting Beijing is only half the responsibility. Washington must also confront weaknesses within the body of laws governing technology access and must weigh in on America's corporate institutions and governance.

America cannot declare artificial intelligence essential to national security while leaving protection of its technology, executives, and policymaking process primarily to corporate discretion. The greatest vulnerability in Silicon Valley may not be the technology we export, but the people and governance systems entrusted to protect it, and the policies determining who gets access to it.

Neither Congress nor the White House should be comfortable leaving that responsibility to Silicon Valley's own moral code.

—By Dewardric McNeal, Managing Director and Senior Policy Analyst at Longview Global, and a CNBC Contributor. He worked in the U.S. Department of Defense as an East Asia and China security relations expert during the Obama administration.

#2894. How Jayson Tatum and the Celtics haven't skipped a beat without Jaylen Brown

Despite having Jayson Tatum for a grand total of 522 minutes during the 2025-26 regular season, the Boston Celtics won nearly 70% of their games, finished in the top five in the NBA in both offensive and defensive efficiency, and landed the No. 2 seed in the Eastern Conference. They reached that level of success thanks, in large part, to Tatum's longtime running mate, Jaylen Brown, turning in the finest individual season of his career en route to a sixth-place finish in MVP voting and an All-NBA Second Team selection.

After that, though, the Celtics traded Brown to the Philadelphia 76ers this summer — a move that you might have heard just a teensy bit about over the past five months, and that has been driving no small amount of conversation ahead of the first meeting of the season between Boston and Philly on Tuesday night.

Since that tectonic roster shift, as they seek to chart a new path forward with a shuffled-up rotation and without a franchise cornerstone, the Celtics have … won 80% of their games, rank in the top seven in the NBA in both offensive and defensive efficiency, and sit in the No. 2 seed in the Eastern Conference.

The more things change, the more they stay the same?

This, to some degree, was the bet that Brad Stevens made when he made the decision to move Brown for Paul George, two first-round picks and two second-round picks. In the longer term, exchanging Brown — who has three years and $185 million remaining on his contract, and is also in line for an extension — for George — who makes less this season, has a player option for next season and comes off the books one year earlier — tidies up the Celtics' balance sheet moving forward, potentially putting them in better position to remain competitive in the race for the top of the East several years down the line. (Say it with me, Brad: "optionality!")

In the here-and-now, though, Stevens wagered that, as excellent as Brown was last season and as vital as he's been to the Celtics' perennial success for the past decade, the surrounding talent and head coach Joe Mazzulla's system would provide enough insulation to ensure that the team wouldn't skip too many beats without Brown. Through 10 games, at least? Boston looks pretty right about that.

The Celtics enter Tuesday's heavyweight battle with the Sixers looking broadly like the same elite squad they were last season. On offense, they sit in or near the top 10 in points scored and allowed per possession, and both team effective field goal percentage and opponents' eFG%. They're great at both avoiding turnovers and forcing them. They grab a ton of offensive rebounds and defend without fouling. They still jack a ton of 3s and make 'em at a top-10 clip, and still give up a ton of 3s in favor of protecting the rim without giving up the entire store on defense. They still play a methodical and effective brand of half-court basketball on offense; they're still great at preventing transition opportunities, despite sending multiple bodies to the offensive glass.

Even without Brown, the fundamentals of who the Celtics are haven't changed; the specifics of how they get there, though, are a little bit different. It starts with Tatum, now 18 months removed from rupturing his Achilles tendon and once again firmly entrenched as Boston's No. 1 option.

Tatum is still ironing out some of the wrinkles in his scoring game, finishing at substandard rates at the rim, from midrange and from beyond the 3-point arc. As he told reporters after blowing out the Charlotte Hornets last week, "I still got a little ways to to get back to being the best I ever felt."

Even with his bucket-getting lagging a bit, though, the 28-year-old is off to a flying start, averaging 27.1 points and 6 rebounds in 33.8 minutes per game. (He's been nails in crunch time, too, ranking eighth in the league in clutch scoring, with 21 points in 15 minutes on 8-for-13 shooting with 3 assists and 0 turnovers.) Just as Brown stepped into a higher-volume role in his absence last season, Tatum has slid into the monster void left by Brown's departure this season. He's finishing a career-high 33.3% of Boston's offensive possessions with a shot attempt, foul drawn or turnover, and leads the Celtics in usage, touches and time of possession.

As Gary Washburn of the Boston Globe put it, "Within the Celtics organization, there appears to be a sense of relief that the tension is gone" — that the team's hierarchy is clarified and established, with Tatum at the top of the pecking order, George comfortably slotting in behind him, and everybody else falling in line after that. Despite that, though, it hasn't felt like Tatum's got a stranglehold of Boston's offense — far from it, in fact.

Tatum's on-ball percentage — literally the share of his floor time that he has the ball in his hands — is down from where it was the past two seasons. His average time of possession of 5.1 minutes per game, 33rd in the NBA, between Donovan Mitchell and Ja Morant.

His willingness to get off the ball rather than pound it has helped produce a more egalitarian attack. Boston is averaging 27.2 assists per game, seventh-most in the NBA, up from 24.6 per game (27th) last season. Those dimes are leading to 70 points per game via assist, sixth-most in the league, up from 64 per game (22nd) last season.

Plenty of those helpers are coming directly off Tatum's fingertips. In the early going of the season, his playmaking has really leaped off the screen. Whether working in the pick-and-roll, operating out of the post, or surveying from the top of the floor at the nail, he's mapping the floor and delivering on-time and on-target feeds more often than ever. Tatum is averaging a career-high 7.8 assists against 2.8 turnovers per game, creating more baskets at the rim than all but five players in the NBA, according to PBP Stats, and setting up more 3-pointers than all but eight. He's one of just two players in the NBA in the top 10 in both scoring and assists. The other? The scorching-hot Luka Dončić.

The player Tatum has assisted the most thus far this season? George, who's been the recipient of 13 Tatum dimes, and who has looked like a hand-in-glove fit in his new digs.

George has picked up where he left off last season, when he came back from a 25-game suspension for violating the NBA/NBPA joint anti-drug program by taking "improper medication" for mental health issues to shine for the Sixers in their playoff push and their first-round victory over these same Celtics. He's averaging 20.4 points, 5.2 rebounds and 4.1 assists in 30.1 minutes per game, shooting 50.7% on 2-pointers, 42.5% on 3-pointers while taking more than eight per game, and 80.8% from the free-throw line.

The nine-time All-Star isn't quite the full-tilt offensive force that he was earlier in his career, but he's fit in brilliantly as a secondary scorer and creator who can demand attention as an off-ball threat on offense, and a high-level, gap-filling playmaker who can help crank up the heat on the defensive end. He's still capable of assuming a larger role when called upon, too: When Tatum got the night off against the Bucks last Wednesday, George stepped to the forefront, scoring 25 points with 5 assists, 4 steals and 3 rebounds to lead the way in a 109-102 win.

"The way we're asking him to play is maybe a little bit different than what he's been asked to do in the past," Mazzulla told reporters in Milwaukee on Sunday. "He enjoys that, he's open-minded to it and he just works at it. He's very hard on himself. Holds himself to a very high standard, constantly watching film, taking notes in his journal and just processing the game. He takes pride in learning and getting better, and you're seeing the fruits of that."

The C's are also seeing the fruits of swapping Brown for George in the possession game. According to Jared Dubin's tracking at Last Night in Basketball, the Celtics finished 15th in the nightly possession battle last season, barely generating more scoring chances than their opposition on a night-to-night basis despite top-five finishes in both offensive rebounding rate and offensive turnover rate — due in large part to bottom-10 marks in both getting to the free-throw line and forcing turnovers on defense. Seven of the 14 teams that finished ahead of Boston last season won a playoff series — including all four conference finalists, and the Sixers team that eliminated them in the first round of the playoffs.

It is perhaps notable, then, that the rate at which the Celtics forced turnovers last season increased more with Brown off the floor than it did for any other player who logged at least 1,000 minutes — and that their forced turnover rate had been significantly higher with him off the floor in each of the previous two seasons.

So: Out went Brown, and in came George, a superior off-ball defender who has consistently posted higher steal rates, and center Mitchell Robinson, who'd built his reputation in New York as one of the league's premier extra-possession creators on offense and a disruptive presence in the middle on defense.

Robinson hasn't yet begun to make a major impact in tilting the math. After a sluggish start to the season following the Knicks' championship run and a short summer, the new arrival has started to round into shape, though, averaging 7 points, 8 rebounds and 2.7 steals-plus-blocks over the last three games, with the C's outscoring opponents by 30 points in his 53 minutes of floor time. All told, though, the Celtics have ratcheted up their board-crashing another notch, pulling in 34% of their missed shots through 10 games, up from 31.8% last season.

They're taking 6.5 more free-throw attempts per game, too, due in large part to a more concerted effort to pressure the rim. Last season, Boston ranked dead last in the share of its field goal attempts that came within four feet of the basket. This season, that's up to 17th.

Tatum's played a big role there. Replacing Brown's high-volume attacking as the only Celtic to rank in the top 50 in the league in drives to the basket per game, he's been living at the free-throw line. His previous career high in trips to the charity stripe was 8.4 per game, accumulated when averaging 36.9 minutes a night during the 2022-23 season. Through his first nine appearances of this season, he's taking 9.3 freebies in 33.8 minutes a night — a notable uptick on a per-minute and per-possession basis, and on pace for far and away the highest free-throw attempt rate of his career. (In fact, Tatum's .477 FTA rate ranks in the top 15 in the league among all non-centers.)

Most notably, though, the C's have been significantly more disruptive on the ball. Last season, Boston ranked 28th in the NBA in steals, 25th in deflections and 23rd in opponent turnover rate, playing more conservatively on defense in the belief that if they just stayed at home, funneled the ball to the areas and shooters they preferred, and contested, the math would work out in their favor over the long run.

This season, though — owing partly to the arrival of George, who's been a demon in the passing lanes in the early going — the Celtics are tied for 14th in steals, fifth in deflections and sixth in opponent turnover rate. They're cashing in on those miscues, too, scoring 20.8 points per game off turnovers — seventh-most in the league, and a full five points per game more than last season. (It's also on pace to be more than any Celtics team has posted since Rick Pitino was on the sidelines at the Garden, regaling reporters about Larry Bird and doors.)

Add up the gains in creating cough-ups, in getting to the line and in even more aggressively crashing the offensive boards, and the Celtics are now generating 4.1 more possessions per game than their opponents — the third-highest total in the league, according to Dubin's tracking.

Thanks to Tatum's playmaking, George's shotmaking and a collective effort from a deep rotation, the Celtics have made those extra possessions count, producing a top-five offense despite shooting just 63.2% at the rim as a team (a bottom-five mark) and seeing multiple players (Tatum, Derrick White, Payton Pritchard, Baylor Scheierman, Jordan Walsh, Hugo González) struggle with frigid jumpers in the early going. If those perimeter players start to thaw out a little bit, this offense could hit a new level, and the Celtics could once again find themselves in the thick of the championship chase, even with Brown now plying his trade elsewhere in the division. The more things change, the more they stay the same.

#2893. Jim Cramer says Anthropic's explosive growth could justify a record-breaking IPO

Anthropic's stunning revenue growth helps justify its $1 trillion-plus valuation ahead of what could become the largest initial public offering in history, CNBC's Jim Cramer said Tuesday.

The Claude developer filed updated IPO paperwork Tuesday outlining plans to offer 825 million shares at $70 to $78 apiece. At the high end, the offering would raise $64.35 billion and value Anthropic at up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis. The company plans to price its shares Nov. 18 and begin trading the following day. Throw in a separate $26 billion private placement purchased by leading AI companies including Nvidia and Alphabet, and Anthropic's entire haul could exceed SpaceX's raise from its June IPO.

"The fact that this deal is on is bullish for tech and the AI trade," the "Mad Money" host said.

Anthropic reported $33.2 billion in revenue during the first nine months of 2026, up 1,206% from a year earlier, according to its updated prospectus. Much of that growth has been driven by enterprise customers, particularly surging adoption of Claude Code, its AI coding agent. "Anthropic's tantalizing growth rate is the key reason why this should be the largest IPO in history," Cramer said.

The company now has more than 11,900 customers spending over $100,000 annually, up from roughly 1,500 at the end of 2025. Existing customers are also spending dramatically more, with Anthropic reporting a net revenue retention rate of approximately 900% at the end of September, meaning customers are spending roughly nine times as much as a year earlier.

While Anthropic is seeing accelerating revenue growth through the first nine months of the year, the company's quarter-over-quarter growth rate seemed to peak in the second quarter, slowing to 47% in the third quarter from 144% in Q2. "To some extent, it's the law of large numbers at work, and let's be clear that 47% sequential growth is still very impressive," Cramer said.

While some deceleration is inevitable as the company gets larger, he said that increased competition from OpenAI and the possibility that customers are beginning to scrutinize their AI spending could also be factors. One thing is for sure, Anthropic won't have to jockey for position with OpenAI, which pushed its IPO plans into next year.

Additionally, Anthropic's annualized revenue run rate, which projects yearly revenue based on the most recent month's sales, suggests demand remains strong. The frontier AI lab reported an annualized revenue run rate of $81 billion at the end of October, putting the company within striking distance of $100 billion by year-end if its current momentum continues.

"It's good to see that it's still growing nicely," Cramer said. "As for Anthropic's revenue and growth rate, the main subject of tonight's review, it's nothing less extraordinary, even if we are slightly past the peak, growth-wise."

#2891. Does 49ers trade for Keenan Allen solidify their Super Bowl hopes?

The San Francisco 49ers acquired veteran wide receiver Keenan Allen in a trade with the Indianapolis Colts for a 2027 sixth-round pick just hours before the NFL trade deadline on Nov. 10.

It's no secret around the NFL that the Niners have a shortage of available wideouts, making the addition of Allen all the more vital to San Francisco's passing game.

The 49ers have been dealt injuries throughout the season Mike Evans, Deebo Samuel Sr., De'Zhaun Stribling, Ricky Pearsall, Demarcus Robinson and Christian Kirk. Each week, there has been someone in the Niners' WR room who steps up to the mantle. Now, Allen gets his turn as San Francisco found their plug-in at receiver.

He returns to the Bay Area having played his collegiate years at Cal from 2010 to 2012. From Cal, he was selected by San Diego Chargers with the 76th overall pick in the third round of the 2013 NFL Draft. Allen was runner-up for Offensive Rookie of the Year but he developed into the Chargers' No. 1-receiver. He would become a six-time Pro Bowl selection in 10 seasons, even as the team moved to Los Angeles.

He spent a season with the Chicago Bears in 2024, then returned to LA for another season with the Chargers. He signed with Indianapolis during the offseason.

With Allen back on the West Coast, the hope is to earn the league's most prized piece of jewelry.

Does Allen have enough left in the tank to help bring a Super Bowl to the Bay Area?

Allen comes to San Francisco as a 34-year-old veteran receiver in his 14th NFL season, which bears the question of if he has enough in the tank to be a contributing piece to the 49ers offense. Allen is added to a star-studded wide receiver room, but still, looming injuries loom are a cause of concern, at least until guys start trending upward.

He'll likely come in as a starter. Allen is known for his elite route-running, high IQ and superb ball-tracking and catching ability. Nothing thrown Allen's way gets dropped.

Allen caught 40 balls for 351 yards and 1 touchdown in eight games with the Colts during the 2026 NFL season. He's shared 22% of targets and has a nearly 69% catch rating. He averages 8.8 yards per catch.

His addition provides Brock Purdy with another weapon to find trust in, especially while the San Francisco receiver room slowly gets healthy. Adding Allen means the team doesn't necessarily have to rush guys back to the field.

Plus, Jordan Watkins is coming off a week where he caught four passes for 86 yards and a touchdown. He continues to improve. Having a threat like Allen alongside Watkins will likely open things up for himself.

The 49ers are still the favorite to win the Super Bowl with +550 odds, according to BetMGM, currently tied with the Buffalo Bills.

For San Francisco, the team's hopes will ride on health and availability. Allen isn't a young buck anymore at 34. Example A, Evans is 33 years old and look how much time he's missed.

No question at what the 49ers will get from the vets on the field, it's a matter of getting them on the field at 100%. Allen has been relatively healthy in 2026 and that's good news for San Francisco.

Allen appeared in every game, except one, for the Colts having missed Week 4 with a groin injury. Since then, he's been dependable, especially production wise. Maybe not the fantasy pickup he once was, but Allen does the things that contributes to winning football games.

#2889. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2888. Rep. Jim Jordan, Sen. John Thune elected GOP leaders in Congress

Republican lawmakers on Tuesday picked Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio and Sen. John Thune of South Dakota to serve as their respective House and Senate minority leaders in the next Congress.

Jordan, the House Judiciary Committee chairman, was elected in a 106-73 vote, sources told MS NOW on condition of anonymity to discuss the private meeting. He beat Rep. Steve Scalise, R-La., the current House majority leader.

A two-decade House veteran and committed supporter of President Donald Trump, Jordan will take on the leadership role just as Republicans relinquish power, following blowout Democratic victories in last week's midterm election.

House Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., announced the day after Election Day that he would not run for minority leader in the 120th Congress.

Thune, in a statement, said he "appreciated the opportunity to have an open, honest, and transparent conversation with my colleagues today, and I am honored to have their continued trust and support to lead the Senate Republican Conference in the 120th Congress."

"I'm proud of what this Republican team has accomplished together, and we remain united in our commitment to advancing conservative priorities, working with President Trump and our colleagues in the House, and holding the line against Democrats' efforts to enact their far-left, liberal agenda," Thune said in the statement.

"We have important work ahead of us, and I look forward to serving alongside my colleagues on behalf of the American people," he said.

#2887. Mississippi Senate: Scott Colom extends tight lead over Hyde-Smith as ballot deadlines hit

Democratic nominee Scott Colom added slightly to his narrow lead over incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate election as two key ballot deadlines hit Tuesday.

Colom, a local district attorney, as of 4:30 p.m. ET, was ahead of Hyde-Smith by 2,607 votes, out of 953,547 votes counted, with an estimated 97% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press. That's a margin of just 0.27 percentage points.

Independent candidate Ty Pinkins had 20,034 votes.

Tuesday is the deadline for resolving affidavit or provisional ballots, which are cast by voters whose eligibility is disputed or unclear on Election Day. It is also the deadline for the receipt of mail-in absentee ballots that are postmarked by Election Day, Nov. 3.

Mississippi is one of just two states whose Senate election has not been called. Alaska is the other.

Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats held by Republicans going into the election. That gives Democrats a new majority in the Senate of at least 54 seats in the 100-member chamber come January.

Alaska before Election Day was viewed as possibly key to the question of whether Republicans would retain their narrow majority in the Senate.

Mississippi, which has not elected a Democratic senator since the segregationist John Stennis in 1982, was on few political analysts' radar as a possible pickup for Democrats. If Colom wins his race, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola is ahead by just 2,517 votes over incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan, with 218,641 total votes counted, a margin of 1.1 percentage points.

Peltola has 48.6% of the total vote. If neither she nor Sullivan gets more than 50% of the total vote, the race will go to a ranked-choice round, in which the two other candidates, Gerald Heikes and another candidate named Dan Sullivan, will be eliminated, and the second choices of voters who made them their first choice will be allocated between Peltola and the incumbent.

#2886. The best revenge for Jaylen Brown is to keep doing what he’s doing

Sunday wasn't the finest outing of the 2026-27 season for the Sixers.

They struggled defensively against the one-win Bulls, but came away with a 127-121 win in Chicago, moving their record to a league-best 9-2. An afternoon day game to close out a three-game road trip against a team collecting ping-pong balls is ripe for a letdown, but the Sixers took care of business.

Jaylen Brown was strong throughout, dropping a game-high 29 points to go along with seven rebounds and four assists. The five-time All-Star's numbers have been solid through 10 games with averages of 22.3 points, 6.7 rebounds and 4.0 assists. He's shooting 47.7% from the field (the exact same percentage he shot last season) with a decent uptick from three at 39.2%.

As the Sixers get set to welcome Jayson Tatum and the Boston Celtics to South Philly on Tuesday, things remain unsettled between Brown and his former organization. When it comes to Tatum, who Brown spent nine seasons with in Boston, there is no beef, but maybe some lamb chops and gravy, as the 2024 Finals MVP put it.

With all the attention and narratives, it's hard to know what we'll see Tuesday night. Will Brown be out for revenge or does he just want to get a win for his team?

There's a way to do both — keep doing exactly what he's doing.

While the numbers have been solid, closely resembling the numbers he put up in 2023-24 when he helped the Celtics win a championship, it's been the little things with Brown.

Sunday's win was a prime example. As the plucky young Bulls kept coming, Brown provided an offensive boost, nailing a pair of threes in the fourth to thwart multiple Chicago runs. With under a minute left, he made all four of his free throws and also came away with a steal on Josh Giddey while the game remained in doubt.

Earlier last week, he made similar plays against the Pistons. While the Sixers were in control throughout the game — with Brown scoring a game-high 30 — a late comeback attempt by Cade Cunningham in Detroit was enough to make any Sixers fan nervous.

A Cunningham layup cut the lead to 106-102. After a rough offensive possession that ended in a Joel Embiid missed three, the Pistons' All-Star guard came charging up the floor. He pulled up for three in transition, but was blocked by Brown. If that shot goes in, it's suddenly a one-point game with 38 seconds left. Instead, Brown's rejection allowed the Sixers to close the game at the free throw line.

While we heard so much talk about Brown's advanced analytics and on/off splits this offseason, it's hard to quantify a guy who simply makes winning plays.

"He's a really good player," Nick Nurse said last week. "He just wins. He defends. He'll rebound. Obviously, he can score from a bunch of different levels, and I'll take all that any day. It's been phenomenal to coach him.

"What does he bring? He brings an intangible of confidence to your team. Even maybe he's not pouring in points or doing anything that you're [seeing in the box score], but there's a level of fight and competitive drive and winning spirit."

Brown has flaws like any other player. Last season, he turned the ball over more than any player in the NBA. This season, he's averaging 2.1 giveaways a game, on pace for his best season in that category since 2018-19.

There were concerns Brown wanted his own team, which supposedly led to the rift with Tatum. Thus far, there have been no issues there. In fact, Brown even openly said last week this was Tyrese Maxey's team and they're all following the second-longest-tenured Sixer's lead.

When Brown is on the floor, the Sixers' net rating is +5.4, per DataBallr. It's -3.1 when he's off. So, it's cut and dry, the Sixers are a better team when Jaylen Brown is on the court.

And just like his time with the Celtics, his current team has an excellent record.

His best path to revenge against his former team is to do what he does — win.

"Listen, the dude is tough and he wins. I'll take that all day long," Nurse said. "Makes a lot of winning plays, man. He's a winner."

#2885. Nvidia sells off for second day in fallout from Jensen Huang’s affair with alleged spy

Nvidia shares sank 5.47% on Tuesday as investors continued to weigh the potential fallout from CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The embattled chip giant briefly dipped below $4 trillion in market cap during afternoon trading, but bounced back to close above that level. Nvidia last cleared the $4 trillion benchmark on July 9, 2025.

Shares closed more than 4% lower on Monday, the day after Huang told staff in an email that he had been "unfaithful" to his wife and had "fallen short" of his responsibilities as CEO.

He reportedly had an affair with Alice Huang, an executive at Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer. The New York Times on Oct. 29 reported that U.S. officials had concluded she was "a Chinese intelligence asset, funneling information to Beijing" and "possibly steering advanced Nvidia A.I. chips to China."

U.S. President Donald Trump said Monday that the FBI is investigating.

"I've been reading a little bit about it over a period of time, but not too long ago," Trump told reporters at the White House. "We'll look into it. I know the FBI is looking into it in terms of something else, and I think that he'll be in good shape."

Adding to the tech giant's woes was a class-action shareholder lawsuit filed against Nvidia and Huang in federal court in San Jose, California.

The Rosen Law Firm's suit alleges the defendants made false or misleading statements about his affair with Huang and Nvidia's relationship with Megaspeed, thereby presenting a material risk to the company's reputation and business prospects.

"Right now the Street and investor chatter is assigning roughly a 15% chance he is removed as CEO of Nvidia with a high probability (85%) he stays on as CEO after the Board investigation is completed," said Dan Ives, senior and managing director at Yorkville Ives & Co, in a Tuesday note.

"We would expect a Board investigation that could take 4 weeks to 6 weeks and will create a clear overhand on the stock until more news emerges from Nvidia."

Amid Nvidia's turmoil, some of its chip rivals are seeing a boost. Advanced Micro Devices and Broadcom built on a strong start to the week, with AMD gaining 1.3% and Broadcom adding nearly 4%. AMD gained 13% on Monday, while Broadcom added just over 10%.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan and Caleigh Keating contributed to this report.

#2884. Winderman’s view: Desperate times? Spoelstra shows in Heat win he’s not waiting

Observations and other notes of interest from Tuesday night's 115-109 victory over the Utah Jazz:

– Giannis Antetokounmpo was questionable 90 minutes before tip-off. He played.

– Andrew Wiggins was reduced to a reserve role for the second time in as many games.

– Myron Gardner and Simone Fontecchio played in the second quarter while Tim Hardaway Jr. remained planted on the bench.

– OK, perhaps not desperate measures, but also no time to fool around.

– Such is the reality of battling back from an 0-4 start to the season.

– Erik Spoelstra could not have sent more specific messages to end the five-game trip.

– Play well, do the right things . . . or don't play.

– Even with a somewhat shaky finish to this one.

– Winning three out of four against the Clippers, Kings and Jazz isn't necessarily something.

– But based on the way the Heat started the season, it also isn't nothing.

– The theme from Spoelstra amid this climb back from the 0-4 start has been small, subtle steps.

– "I see some things trending in a better direction," he said. "And these little mini victories that you have an opportunity for, you want to seize those opportunities."

– For the second consecutive game, the Heat opened with a lineup of Antetokounmpo, Bam Adebayo, Klay Thompson, Davion Mitchell and Pelle Larsson.

– For the first time this season, Keshad Johnson was active for the Heat on his two-way contract.

– Tre Donaldson and Vlad Goldin again were inactive on their two-way deals.

– The very-shorthanded Jazz opened with a lineup of Darryn Peterson , Josh Green , Josh Okogie, Ace Bailey and Jaxson Hayes.

– Among those sidelined for the Jazz were Keyonte George, Jaren Jackson Jr., Lauri Markkanen and Jusuf Nurkic.

– So basically four of their five starters and four of their six-best players.

– With Utah short on big bodies, Jazz coach Will Hardy spoke pregame of the challenge ahead.

– "For years with Giannis, the point of contact is always so important because you can keep the ball in front but drop too far back," he said. "Bam also commands a lot of respect in terms of we need to show multiple bodies. So it's going to be like every night. It's a team defense night. Recognizing some of the size differential in different matchups, we're going to need to show those secondary bodies a little bit earlier."

– It helped the Jazz that Antetokounmpo got into early foul trouble.

– Of Hardaway attempting to find his way back amid limited minutes, Spoelstra said, "I understand it, and it is tough. Some of these decisions are tough for me. It's tough for some of the guys to find their rhythm."

– Spoelstra added, "Be a positive on the scoreboard, change the energy of the game, and then we'll sort through rhythm, touches, getting guys playing comfortable."

– During the opening period, the Jazz lost a challenge and even more.

– The Jazz challenged a charging foul drawn by Mitchell. Upon review, referee Scott Foster said the ball was deflected before the block/charge contact.

– So instead of the original charge call on the Jazz and Heat possession, the Heat were awarded two free throws on the loose-ball foul.

– Shades of Dick Bavetta. So Jazz challenge . . . gives Heat two free throws.

– Spoelstra said among the goals in getting the defense back on track is a team-wide commitment against the pick-and-roll.

– "The mentality and mindset is five guys attacking the pick-and-roll," he said. "It has to be that. It can't just be the two players involved in the pick-and-roll, because there's going to be a lot of other weak side things that happen from that."

– On that theme, Adebayo said of the pick-and-roll defense, "For us, it's getting our coverages right, making the right calls and believing that we can get deflections and steals."

– Of opponents loading up on 3-pointers this season against the Heat, Adebayo said it's a whole new world in that regard,

– "I mean guys, we've seen it from 30 feet," he said of a distance nearly seven-feet beyond the arc. "So it's a longer closeout game than it was four or five years ago. So for us, it's understanding that there are longer closeouts and understanding that we've got to get out there."

– Going in, the Heat's numbers with Adebayo on the floor and Antetokounmpo off were actually better offensively than defensively, which assuredly would not have been expected.

#2883. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2882. CoreWeave beats on revenue and lifts guidance, easing AI slowdown concerns

CoreWeave reported better-than-expected quarterly revenue and lifted its 2026 guidance on Tuesday, showing that demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite concerns of a market slowdown. The stock advanced in extended trading.

The earnings announcement lands at a particularly volatile moment for CoreWeave and its neocloud peers, as the market sours on the AI trade and as Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the central figure in the global buildout, faces a scandal tied to an affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

CoreWeave's revenue soared 168% from about $1.36 billion a year earlier, according to a statement. The company recorded a net loss of $709 million, or $1.27 per share, compared to a net loss of $110 million a year earlier. Adjusted earnings exclude stock-based compensation.

Executives pushed up their forecast for 2026. They now see $1.275 billion to $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income on $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by Visible Alpha were looking for $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income, and analysts polled by LSEG had expected $12.86 billion in 2026 revenue.

In August, CoreWeave's 2026 guidance included $960 million to $1.15 billion in adjusted operating income and $12.4 billion to $13.2 billion in revenue.

CoreWeave has lost over 40% of its value in the past month, as investors rotate out of companies most closely tied to AI infrastructure. Rising interest rates have pushed lenders out of the market, raising concerns about ongoing access to capital. Some CoreWeave bond yields recently topped 16%.

"The business can execute well and still face a tougher financing environment," Joe Tigay, portfolio manager at Equity Armor Investments, wrote in an email. "Strong demand is encouraging; the cost and availability of capital remain critical."

CoreWeave will get most the capital it needs for 2027 through clients pre-payments and financing from hardware partners, and the company will raise "a limited amount" as well, CEO Mike Intrator told analysts on a conference call.

The company plans to raise new debt tied to a set of contracts in the weeks to come, said Nitin Agrawal, its finance chief.

"At current yields, we do not intend to issue additional parent-level unsecured debt through 2027," he said. "By contrast, the asset-level secured debt market has remained stable, with our loans continuing to trade around par value." In all, he said he projects under $5 billion in parent-level debt financing through 2027.

Fellow neocloud Nebius, whose stock is down by a similar amount, is scheduled to report earnings on Thursday. Nebius shares got a small boost from CoreWeave's results.

Both companies and several other providers of cloud-based graphics processing units are particularly reliant on Nvidia, which provides GPUs as well as necessary capital to the whole ecosystem. Nvidia shares have dropped nearly 10% to start the week as the Huang affair raises pressure on the company in Washington, D.C. Nvidia's board said on Tuesday that it hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to investigate Huang and determine whether national security or company secrets were compromised.

CoreWeave investors are also looking toward next week, when key customer Anthropic is scheduled to debut on the Nasdaq. In its updated IPO prospectus on Tuesday, Anthropic indicated that it could be valued at around $1.4 trillion after its share sale. Anthropic has signed deals for the construction of entire data centers that will meet its needs. "We see that we're going to have long-term relationships with the AI labs," said Intrator, a CoreWeave co-founder.

During the quarter, CoreWeave said it would establish its first data centers in Asia and announced the Forge service for running and evaluating AI models.

Management still anticipates 1.85 gigawatts in active power by the end of 2026. CoreWeave is targeting $37 billion to $39 billion in full-year capital expenditures, which would imply 155% growth at the middle of the range. Analysts polled by Visible Alpha were looking for $37.69 billion.

CoreWeave owed almost $47 billion in debt at the end of the third quarter, according to the statement. Like its bigger cloud rivals such as Amazon and Oracle, CoreWeave is rushing to bolster computing capacity for clients.

But last week, voters in the U.S., where CoreWeave derives most of its revenue, rejected dozens of initiatives on data center development. In Texas, Democrat Gina Hinojosa defeated the incumbent, Republican Greg Abbott, after she said Abbott should try to end 20-year tax breaks on large data centers.

"So to begin with, we do not anticipate any impact from the seismic regulatory shift that's occurred," Intrator said.

Almost all of CoreWeave's power is online or is permitted and under construction, he said.

"If data center construction across the sector were to slow, installed compute becomes scarcer, and the knock-on effect is that GPU pricing may stay higher for longer," Intrator said.

He said the company does not expect risks to deliveries in 2027 based on construction at permitted locations.

"We could sell all of the capacity that we have to current buyers that are in the queue. We are choosing not to," Intrator said. We're choosing to be patient. We're choosing to bring the infrastructure to market when it is further along, because we believe that that will result in better economic output for the outstanding infrastructure that we have."

Meanwhile, demand remains robust, with the company now sitting on a $132.5 billion revenue backlog, up from $104 billion as of June.

"Should we choose to focus only on our committed backlog, we would expect capex for 2027 to remain in line with our 2026 capex investment, while revenue and EBITDA grow significantly," Agrawal said.

#2880. 10-year Treasury yield races back to 5% as inflation data, Waller comments drive rate hike expectations

Treasury yields jumped on Tuesday after the U.S. consumer price index data for October came in hotter than expected. A mediocre auction of $42 billion in Treasury notes compounded the decline in bonds.

The yield on the 10-year Treasury note — the benchmark for mortgages, car loans and credit card rates — traded above 5% before paring gains. It was last up 13 basis points at 4.992%. The 30-year Treasury bond yield, more sensitive to geopolitical developments, rose more than 9 basis points to 5.352%. The 2-year Treasury note yield, which closely tracks short-term Fed rate expectations, surged more than 18 basis points to 4.736%.

The 10-year yield last traded above the 5% level earlier this month. In October, 10-year and 30-year yields hit 24-year highs.

One basis point equals 0.01% or 1/100th of 1%, and yields and prices move inversely to one another.

The consumer price index for October grew 0.4%, more than the 0.3% expected from economists polled by Dow Jones and down from the 0.6% growth in September.

Core CPI, which excludes volatile food and energy prices, gained 0.3%. Economists had expected a 0.2% gain on the month. The reading was in line with September's 0.3% rise.

The report came after Fed Governor Christopher Waller said he would support "more robust action" if inflation readings are higher than expected.

"In that case, I believe a 50 basis point hike in the policy rate should be considered at the December meeting," Waller said in London at a central bank conference hosted by the Bank of England. "Hiking only 25 basis points would put our credibility at risk, a risk I am not eager to take."

Lindsay Rosner, head of multi-sector fixed income investing at Goldman Sachs Asset Management, said the hot CPI numbers signal that Fed hikes could spill into 2027.

"The Fed could push for a more-aggressive tightening cycle with CPI continuing to print strong," she said. "A December hike is our firm base case despite wider AI jitters, and stubborn inflation means we cannot rule out further hikes in 2027."

A lackluster auction of 10-year Treasurys added further downward pressure on bond prices Tuesday. "Today's 10-year auction was a bit soft," BMO Capital economists wrote. "Before the auction, Treasuries were selling off on strong volumes, and 10-year notes went into the bidding deadline 12 bp cheaper on the day. Since the results, yields have ticked slightly higher in the follow-through."

"[D]ealers were left with 10% of the auction, the most since May," said Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners. "Inflation breakevens today are focused on the drop in oil prices while regular Treasuries today are reacting to a Fed that is definitely going to hike next month, as of now, with one another in January," he said.

Oil prices dipped further Tuesday, with Brent crude falling below $90 per barrel for the first time since August. Brent futures settled above that level, however, falling 1.17% to at $91.49 a barrel. This comes on the back of President Donald Trump's comments that the war with Iran could end "fairly soon" and downplaying the prospect of further strikes in the region.

Tuesday's CPI data, together with wholesale inflation numbers due Friday, may shape the Federal Reserve's future decisions on interest rates. Markets have largely priced in a quarter percentage point hike in December, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool. Friday's stronger-than-expected U.S. employment data, more than double what economists had forecast, further strengthened those hawkish expectations.
