SALT LAKE CITY – Against another opponent on another night, the Miami Heat's momentum might have stalled. Instead the perfect opponent to end a five-game, 10-day trip.

Against a team that entered 1-9 and was without four of five starters, the Heat made it three victories in their last four games with a 115-109 victory Tuesday night over the Utah Jazz at Delta Center.

While it wasn't as easy as expected, and while the record still is underwater at 5-6, the victory completed a 3-2 western swing for Erik Spoelstra's team, with a five-game homestand to follow.

Not only did the Jazz enter with the league's worst record, but they were without starters Keyonte George, Jaren Jackson Jr., Lauri Markkanen and Jusuf Nurkic.

So while it was somewhat shaky, the Heat did (barely) enough, even with Giannis Antetokounmpo battling an ongoing wrist issue and this time foul trouble, as well.

With Antetokounmpo still working his way to 30 points and 11 rebounds, the Heat had enough against an opponent that got 25 points from No. 2 2026 draft pick Darryn Peterson and 23 from Ace Baily.

In support of Antetokounmpo, the Heat got 19 points, eight assists and five rebounds from Davion Mitchell, on a night Bam Adebayo was held to 11 points along with eight rebounds.

Five Degrees of Heat from Tuesday night's game:

1. Game flow: The Heat led 29-25 after the first period. They then pushed their lead to 17 in the second period, and then went into halftime up 64-54, unable to build more of a lead despite 11 first-half Jazz turnovers.

And even then, it required another transition block, this time by Adebayo, when the Jazz threatened to close within three with 22.1 seconds to play.

2. Waiting game: Antetokounmpo had been downgraded from probable to questionable 90 minutes before tip due to his sprained right wrist that has been taped postgame recently.

Of the initial indecision, Spoelstra said, "We just have to evaluate where he is, just like we would with anybody else."

The calculus with Antetokounmpo nonetheless changed when he was called for his second foul with 5:33 left in the opening period, forcing Adebayo to quickly return amid his first rest of the night.

Antetokounmpo then iced his wrist while on the bench.

Antetokounmpo then was called for his third foul with 6:33 left in the second period, a call Spoelstra unsuccessfully challenged, nonetheless leaving Antetokounmpo in the game at that stage.

With his eighth point, Antetokounmpo passed Gary Payton (21,813) for 39th place on the NBA all-time scoring list.

Antetokounmpo closed 12 of 21 from the field, 5 of 9 from the line, with free throws remaining an ongoing Heat issue.

#2878. Week 10 Fantasy Football Waiver Wire: Corey Kiner, Tank Dell among top pickups

Every Monday during the season, Justin Boone breaks down the recommended waiver-wire pickups.

Roster percentages are generated from Yahoo leagues. Free-Agent Budget (FAB) amounts are based on a $100 salary cap. Only players rostered in less than 50% of leagues are considered.

With Kirk Cousins heading back to Minnesota, the Raiders can finally put their first-overall pick under center. While you're not going to want to use Mendoza against the Seahawks defense this week, his outlook for the rest of the season is quite promising as a highly-drafted pick attached to Klint Kubiak.

The Heisman Trophy winner proved himself to be a good leader in college with excellent on-field awareness and the toughness needed to overcome adversity during games. He has a strong arm to make any throw, but it's his touch passing that stood out. Though Mendoza's not fast enough to be a meaningful runner in the pros, he has enough speed to scramble his way out of trouble and pick up yards on the ground. This all amounts to an intriguing fantasy QB2 with room to grow.

The quarterback position is very picked over at the moment. Malik Willis was featured in this spot last week, but he's now playing through a groin injury that's limited his abilities as a runner and neutered his fantasy potential. So, all that's left are uncomfortable streaming options.

The Steelers are coming off their bye week, which should help the 42-year-old Rodgers (turns 43 in December) feel refreshed for a date with the division rival Bengals.

Rodgers hasn't had many favorable opponents this season, but he did take advantage of his easiest matchup. That just happened to be against the Bengals in Week 3, when the veteran passed for 292 yards and three touchdowns en route to a QB8 finish.

In his last visit to Cincinnati, Rodgers threw four touchdowns back in Week 7 of the 2025 campaign. He's not a slam-dunk streamer, but he's a solid one given the options this week.

Desperation streaming options (if healthy)

Desperation streaming options (if starter is out)

The elite backup RBs who are one injury away from stardom should be rostered everywhere and I'll continue to highlight them in this space until they no longer qualify for this article.

It was clear early in the season that Johnson was one of the best backups to roster, in case anything ever happens to Kenneth Walker III. In recent weeks, Johnson's value has increased even more now that he's offering weekly flex production.

The rookie posted a fluky 128 receiving yards and a score in Week 8, but he followed it up with 34 yards and another touchdown on Sunday. Upcoming matchups with the Falcons, Cardinals and Bills are all spots where Johnson could be a viable flex play while also offering league-winning upside if Walker ever misses time.

Black is in the same situation as Johnson; you can basically just swap Walker's name with Christian McCaffrey's in the previous section.

The 49ers' rookie plays in one of the best offenses in the league and has flashed his potential with at least 60 rushing yards in three of his first eight games, while serving as CMC's direct backup. That includes the past two games, when Black turned 12 touches into 63 yards and a TD against the Falcons, as well as Sunday's outing, which resulted in 11 carries for a season-high 70 yards.

Next up is the Cowboys defense, which is a top-five matchup for fantasy running backs. The 49ers are 4-point favorites in a game with the highest projected point total in Week 10 at 55.5.

Stashing a talented No. 2 back playing behind a 30-year-old starter who handled 450 touches last year is just an obvious move. Black should be rostered everywhere as a flex option with RB1 upside if McCaffrey were ever sidelined.

Rhamondre Stevenson left Sunday's game with a knee injury and was very quickly ruled out. Mike Vrabel didn't have an update Monday morning, but did acknowledge that his back was undergoing an MRI. With TreVeyon Henderson already sidelined indefinitely, that leaves Kiner as the next man up.

Though Kiner hasn't been asked to do much as a pass-catcher, the 2025 UDFA has shown some juice in the run game by topping 50 yards in three of his last five outings in a change-of-pace role.

If Stevenson can't suit up in Week 10, which seems unlikely due to news that he'll probably miss some time, Kiner immediately becomes a high-end fantasy RB3 with the usual touchdown upside that comes with a starter's workload. We can't lock that outcome in, though until we get a proper update on Stevenson's injury.

Josh Jacobs fractured his finger and is set to undergo surgery that will keep him out a maximum of three games.

MarShawn Lloyd missed Week 9 due to a knee injury, which allowed Johnson to step up in Jacobs' absence. Johnson finished with 68 yards and a touchdown, making him the RB11 on the week with just MNF left to be played.

If you're wondering why Johnson isn't higher on this list, just take a look at Green Bay's upcoming schedule. The Packers face the Vikings this week, then have a bye before taking on the Rams.

So, even with Jacobs sidelined, this isn't the ideal setup for a spot starter. On top of that, we aren't sure if/when Lloyd will be available. If Lloyd is active in Week 10, it only further muddies the projections.

If Lloyd remains out, Johnson would be a risky RB3/flex versus the Vikings. If Lloyd plays, then both players would be risky flex options.

With the Saints cutting Kendre Miller, Estimé stepped into a significant role in his first game since joining the Saints. The result was a 47% snap share, which led to 15 carries for 72 yards and a touchdown, as well as another five yards on a catch.

Estimé's presence helped kickstart New Orleans' rushing attack, but his fantasy outlook is directly tied to Travis Etienne Jr.'s health. Etienne, who can be activated off IR at any time, wasn't ready to practice last week. Once we see him back on the field, it will severely diminish Estimé's fantasy value.

However, if Etienne misses another week, fantasy managers will be able to start Estimé as a fringe low-end RB2.

It's easy to forget, but Bryant had a growing role in the Broncos' passing attack during the opening month before being placed on injured reserve with an ankle injury. In those first four games, Bryant topped 40 yards three times.

Deebo Samuel Sr. is nearing a return from a hamstring injury that kept him out for the last month. Mike Evans' Achilles tendinitis is an ongoing problem with no clear recovery timeline. If everything remains the same with Evans and Deebo sitting out, Watkins will be a boom-or-bust WR4/flex option in a good matchup with the Cowboys this week. However, once Allen is up to speed and the other receivers are healthy, Watkins won't be worth rostering in redraft leagues.

#2876. Here's the inflation breakdown for October 2026 — in one chart

Inflation throttled back in October from its blistering pace in September amid a more moderate rise in gasoline prices, but remained high as categories like food and housing put upward pressure on consumer prices.

The consumer price index, an inflation barometer, rose 0.4% on a monthly basis in October, after adjusting for seasonal factors, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday. That's down from 0.6% in September but higher than the 0.3% that economists expected.

"It's uncomfortably high," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's. "The Fed's not going to be happy about it. It certainly makes it more likely we get another rate increase in December."

The Federal Reserve uses inflation data to guide its interest rate policy. The U.S. central bank raised rates in September amid a recent pickup in inflation, which economists largely attribute to the war in Iran. It held rates steady at its last meeting in October.

Economists generally assess a measure known as "core" CPI — which strips out volatile food and energy prices — to examine underlying inflation dynamics in the U.S. economy.

That core metric rose 0.3% in October, as it also did in September and August. A consistent monthly reading around 0.2% would be more in line with the Fed's 2% annual inflation target, Zandi said.

Inflation has been above the Fed's target for more than five years.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics didn't report an annual inflation figure in the latest CPI report because of the lengthy federal government shutdown that happened a year ago. That shutdown, which ran from Oct. 1 to Nov. 12, disrupted the agency's typical data collection, and it didn't release an inflation report for October 2025.

"Investors do not need year-over-year figures to see that this was a hot inflation report and that prices are not on a sustainable path back toward the Fed's 2% target," Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth management firm, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

"It would have taken exceptional evidence of disinflation to dissuade the Fed from raising rates in December, and this report delivered the opposite," he wrote.

Economists pointed to four big reasons for the hotter-than-expected CPI reading.

Perhaps the largest contributor to the outsized inflation reading in Tuesday's CPI report was housing, Zandi said.

Housing is the largest component of CPI, and movements in the category strongly influence the index's month-to-month reading, he said.

The "shelter" CPI index rose 0.3% in October, accounting for over 30% of the CPI increase, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Zandi said he doesn't expect that dynamic to persist, however, because it's not consistent with the trajectory of market rents in the U.S.

"It does feel like we have a high level of monthly volatility" in the CPI data, he said. "It's all over the map month to month."

But economists said broader factors are putting upward pressure on prices, too.

"Core inflation today reflects three outsized shocks: tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related demand," Josh Hirt, senior U.S. economist at Vanguard Group, an asset manager, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

The Iran war has raised oil prices, pushing up prices for fuels like gasoline, diesel and jet fuel, which are refined from crude oil, economists said.

Gasoline prices rose 1% in October, a significant pullback after a 9.1% monthly increase in September, CPI data showed.

While diesel prices have retreated from recent record highs, they remain elevated and could spill more broadly into the U.S. economy in the coming months, economists said.

"Diesel prices have a really slow pass-through to all the areas of CPI, given it affects transportation costs," said Thomas Ryan, a senior North America economist at Capital Economics.

"These things take time," Ryan said. "It'll be an inflationary impetus for at least the next three to six months, I suspect."

There was some evidence of that broader percolation of diesel prices in October, such as in food prices, Zandi said. Higher diesel prices add to the cost of transporting food to the grocery store shelves, he said.

Food prices rose 0.5% in October after an unchanged reading in September, according to CPI data.

The run-up in jet fuel costs has also led airlines to raise their ticket prices, economists said. Airfares climbed 0.4% in October, after monthly increases of 1.2%, 2.7% and 3.8% in the preceding months, respectively, according to the CPI report.

Meanwhile, the inflationary impact of tariffs imposed by the Trump administration should fade by the end of the year, assuming the White House doesn't renew its push for more import taxes, Zandi said.

Certain categories of physical goods, like toys, furniture, and appliances, saw elevated inflation rates in October, likely because businesses have passed on tariff costs to consumers, he said.

The buildout of data centers that power artificial intelligence has driven up prices for electricity and consumer electronics, economists said.

Those data centers have led to a shortage of technology equipment, such as memory chips used in household electronic products like iPhones, Ryan said.

#2875. Yaxel Lendeborg hilariously states how he will honor ‘glorious king' Steph Curry

Yaxel Lendeborg hilariously states how he will honor 'glorious king' Steph Curry originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

It's only has taken 11 games for Yaxel Lendeborg to become a full-fledged Steph Curry truther.

On the online platform Kick, where Lendeborg streams under the name LordLendeborg, the Golden State rookie humorously stated he'd change his room's decorations to celebrate Curry.

"Y'all see the GOAT over there," Lendeborg said, pointing to a poster on his wall of Michael Jordan shooting a free throw. "I'm gonna change it to a Steph Curry poster, guys. Don't worry, my real goat. My glorious king. My one and only."

Lendeborg, like nearly all of Curry's teammates, has heaped praise on the two-time MVP since getting on the court with him.

The 24-year-old said it would be an "honor" to watch Curry in person and added he "caught [him]self shaking [his] head, like 'wow' a couple times," after Curry scored 44 points in a five-point win over the Minnesota Timberwolves on Oct. 28.

Since getting selected with the No. 11 overall pick in the 2026 NBA Draft, Lendeborg's impact on the Warriors has been undeniable.

Lendeborg is averaging 21.4 points and 7.2 rebounds per game, shooting 52.3 percent from the field and 44.3 percent from the 3-point line, firmly positioning himself as a contender in the Rookie of the Year race.

The Michigan alumnus has rejuvenated Golden State's veteran core, with forward Draymond Green calling Lendeborg a "special player" and Curry labeling him the "full package."

The Warriors are in the midst of an eight-game winning streak that has pushed them to fourth in the Western Conference standings, and Lendeborg will continue to be a huge part of the team's success this season.

#2874. Hurricanes Embrace Finnish Culture Before NHL Global Series

Finnish music began playing during the Carolina Hurricanes' activities in Helsinki.

It wasn't a random choice, but an initiative by players and staff members born in Finland who used their visit to connect more closely with local customs.

The gesture reflects the atmosphere around the team during a week when hockey shares the spotlight with food, traditions, and family reunions, before facing the Seattle Kraken in the NHL Global Series Finland 2026.

Sebastian Aho, born in Rauma; Jesperi Kotkaniemi, a native of Pori; and Juuso Välimäki, from Tampere, took on the role of hosts for their teammates. The three Finns have the opportunity to show a side of their country that is normally distant for those who build their careers in North America.

"As Europeans, we are always in their world, and there are definitely things that are different. Food and drinks are part of what we can offer them and tell them: 'Hey, this is good, try it,'" Välimäki explained, highlighting the group's willingness to take part in local activities.

The defenseman also shared an anecdote that illustrates the delegation's enthusiasm for discovering new customs. "Even this morning, the strength coaches and the guys were saying: 'Let's put on some Finnish music for once.' They are small details, but I think it's great. They are willing to live it fully and experience everything," added the 28-year-old player.

The bonding began on the first night, when Aho organized an outing and a dinner for the members of the organization. Although the day was mainly dedicated to spending time off the ice, the schedule also includes other experiences that will help them learn some particularities of Finland.

Food options include traditional salmon soup and reindeer meat, two specialties that sparked curiosity among the visitors.

"Last night was more about sharing as a team and spending time together. I think today we have plans to try Finnish food. We have some activities scheduled, and I understand there will be salmon soup and also reindeer, so we are definitely going to try them," said defenseman Sean Walker.

Shayne Gostisbehere also expressed his willingness to follow the hosts' recommendations, as he is taking part in his third NHL regular-season international series.

"We are excited. Basically, right now, if the Finns tell us to jump, we ask how high. We are really looking forward to trying their food and traditions," said the American, who also mentioned a planned visit to a sauna establishment.

For the veteran, these experiences offer a chance to get to know his teammates better, especially when they can share customs that are part of their identity. "It's special to see them play in their own country, but also that they can share their culture with us. That is something very beautiful," Gostisbehere added.

Coach Rod Brind'Amour, for his part, humorously acknowledged that his plans are much simpler than the players'. The coach said the coaching staff usually stays together and looks for restaurants near the hotel, although he left open the possibility of exploring during free time.

"We're not that adventurous anymore. Those days are behind us. We always go out together and go to the restaurant closest to the hotel. If we find a good one around the corner, we'll probably go back tonight," he joked.

However, the coach also acknowledged that a visit of this nature deserves to be enjoyed beyond sporting obligations. "We have about a day and a half to find something to do. If anyone has suggestions, let me know. But you also have to enjoy it. When you come all the way here, you have to take advantage of the experience," he said.

Among the players especially enjoying the stay in Helsinki is Nikolaj Ehlers, a forward born in Denmark who knows the city from previous international commitments. The attacker took part in the 2018 Global Series with the Winnipeg Jets and also represented his country at the 2022 World Championship.

"I have been here several times, also with the national team at some World Championships. I like Helsinki; I even stayed at the same hotel last time. I know the city a little, so I will try to help the guys who need it, although we have three Finns who will do most of the work," he said with a laugh.

Nevertheless, the most important aspect of this new experience will be the possibility of reuniting with close people who normally cannot accompany him during the NHL calendar, and the player explained that several family members and friends will travel from Denmark, taking advantage of a considerably easier flight connection than the one needed to travel to the United States.

"It is definitely easier. From my hometown to here, with a stop in Copenhagen, it is about four hours or four and a half hours. I have family and friends coming, including my two nephews and my niece, so it will be exciting to see them," Ehlers said.

For Brind'Amour, that circumstance adds another dimension to the games against Seattle, as he considers that international commitments offer unusual opportunities for Europeans.

"We have three Finnish guys, and we know how important this is for them, but Ehlers will also have his friends and family nearby. There is a lot to play for. Obviously, they are NHL games, but there is another story behind all of this," the coach explained.

The coach even stressed that the team has a special responsibility to those who will be able to perform in front of their loved ones. "We have to play for these guys. It is not something that happens once in a lifetime, but almost. I hope we can do well," he added.

#2873. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2872. Singapore wants a bigger slice of the global gold market. Is it too late to the party?

Singapore is stepping up efforts to become a major player in the global gold market, betting that a new central bank vaulting service and tax incentives will help it attract more bullion flows.

The Monetary Authority of Singapore on Tuesday launched a gold vaulting service for foreign central banks and sovereign entities, with an initial capacity of 500 metric tons.

The city-state also plans to introduce tax exemptions for certain wholesale gold-related services such as vaulting, refining, recasting, testing and broking. In June, Singapore unveiled plans for a physical gold clearing system to facilitate the settlement of bullion transactions in the city-state, with operations expected to begin in the first quarter of 2027.

The initiatives mark the country's latest attempt to establish itself as a key bullion hub alongside established centers such as London and Switzerland, as more gold trading activity shifts toward Asia.

But breaking into a market dominated by established trading hubs may prove an uphill battle. For instance, London vaults held about 9,850 metric tons of gold at the end of October, including commercial and central bank storage.

The push from Singapore also comes as rival Hong Kong is also expanding its gold storage and clearing infrastructure, leveraging its proximity to China, the world's largest gold market.

"Singapore is very late to the party as building up a physical hub requires volume when it comes to trade backed structures," said Juerg Kiener, chief investment officer at Swiss Asia Capital.

"The largest gold market is China and its window to the rest of the world is Hong Kong," he told CNBC, adding that Singapore is not well-positioned to compete with Hong Kong in gold-backed trade.

Kiener said Singapore could instead carve out a niche in physical gold storage for financial institutions, although commercial vaulting services are already available in the city-state, including at its high-security freeport near Changi Airport.

Unlike commercial vaults, the new facility will be operated by Singapore's central bank, offering foreign monetary authorities an alternative sovereign custodian for their gold.

"It's not something that we see happening all that often in the gold market or in the central bank community," said Shaokai Fan, global head of central banks at the World Gold Council.

The move comes as central banks increasingly reassess where they store their bullion amid geopolitical tensions, said Fan. Over the past 12 months, several countries have diversified gold storage, with many planning to do so, according to a World Gold Council report published in September.

Singapore's political stability, strong legal framework and extensive air connectivity could make it an attractive alternative to traditional storage centers, he added.

Nikolas Kavalis, managing director at Metals Focus, said the sovereign vaulting service represents an important addition to Singapore's existing gold infrastructure, but cautioned that storage alone would not be sufficient to establish a fully functioning bullion market.

The bigger challenge is building sufficient liquidity and trading activity to compete with London, which remains the dominant global over-the-counter gold market.

Both Singapore and Hong Kong are still developing their local clearing infrastructure, Kavalis said, meaning neither has yet established the kind of deep trading ecosystem that underpins London's dominance.

Attracting central bank gold could help Singapore build that liquidity, particularly if bullion stored locally can also be mobilized for transactions rather than simply held in custody.

Singapore's efforts come as gold flows have already undergone a significant transformation over the past decade.

Historically, much of the world's bullion moved through London and Switzerland, where large gold bars were traded or refined before being shipped to Asian markets.

But the expansion of Asian refineries and growing regional demand have increasingly allowed gold to move directly between Asian markets, reducing their dependence on European trading centers, Kavalis said.

The development of more sophisticated gold hubs in Asia could accelerate that shift.

Still, Hong Kong might have an edge in serving central banks and institutions whose financial activities are closely tied to China, while Singapore could appeal to those already active in its banking and financial markets, Kavalis said.

Fan sees room for both centers to grow. "We see the two centers complementing each other and not really competing with each other," he said.

Singapore's Deputy Prime Minister Gan Kim Yong similarly dismissed suggestions that the two financial hubs were looking at securing a larger share of the same pie. "It's not a zero-sum game," Gan said Tuesday, adding that Singapore's vaulting service would provide central banks with another option for diversifying where they hold their reserves.

Whether Singapore can translate its reputation as a trusted financial center into meaningful bullion flows remains to be seen. "It's a bit chicken and egg because to attract volumes you want liquidity, but to get liquidity you need volumes," Fan said.

#2868. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2867. White House vows to press ahead with tariffs despite midterm backlash over rising prices

One week after voters punished Republicans over rising prices in the midterm elections, President Donald Trump's White House signaled it would stay the course on tariffs, even as his former top economic advisor warned that Americans are footing the bill.

Jim Goyer, Trump's director of White House Office of Public Liaison, defended tariffs as a tool to rebuild U.S. manufacturing, secure supply chains and protect national security during an address Tuesday at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"And we are just getting started," Goyer said. "Our ambition is to continue this pace for the remainder of the president's term in office."

But Gary Cohn, who served as Trump's chief economic advisor during his first administration, offered a sharply different assessment during an onstage interview later Tuesday.

"Tariffs are a consumption tax on the U.S. consumer," Cohn said, arguing that the duties are raising prices and forcing Americans to reconsider purchases.

The divide exposed a growing dilemma for Trump. His trade agenda is reshaping global commerce – and driving up costs for voters. Companies are adapting to the chaos, but the old trading system isn't coming back.

"President Trump has disrupted that system in a way that we're not likely to return to," Kevin Brady, a former Republican congressman who helped lead the B20 summit, said during a discussion with U.S. Chamber CEO Suzanne Clark.

The political backlash was evident in the Nov. 3 election. Roughly two-thirds of voters said Trump's policies contributed to inflation, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Nearly nine in 10 said the cost of living was a major or crucial factor in their vote.

A separate Reuters/Ipsos poll released Tuesday found 62% of Americans supported repealing Trump's tariffs, while just 21% approved of his handling of the cost of living.

"Last week, American voters sent a clear message about the cost of living and their economic security," Clark said in her keynote address.

The pressure intensified Tuesday as new government data showed consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, exceeding economists' expectations. Annual inflation reached 3.7% in September, the latest available year-over-year figure and well above the Federal Reserve's 2% target.

Tariffs are not the only factor driving higher prices. The war with Iran has pushed energy costs sharply higher, while housing, healthcare and other household expenses continue to strain consumers.

But research has found Trump's import duties have added to those pressures. A Federal Reserve Bank of New York study estimated that about 26% of tariff increases were passed through to consumer prices, with some effects taking nine to 12 months to work through supply chains.

Cohn, now vice chairman of IBM, argued that while tariffs generate revenue for the U.S., those gains come at consumers' expense.

"You're looking at one side of the equation. You're looking at the credit side of the balance sheet," Cohn said, describing how he would respond to Trump's defense of tariff revenue. "There's the debit side of the balance sheet, which is every hardworking American who happened to pay the tariff through some product that they bought."

Still, Cohn saw room for a bipartisan trade deal. He suggested Trump and the incoming Democratic Congress could make protections against China and unfair trade practices permanent while rolling back tariffs on Canada, leaving less for future presidents to undo.

For businesses, the shift is already underway. UPS CEO Carol Tomé said customers are moving production and sourcing closer to home as global supply chains become more regional.

"Supply chains are diversifying. Supply chains are regionalizing," Tomé said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Morgan Brennan.

Separately, UPS is opening a new $80 million-plus air hub in the Philippines in October to expand its Asian network and improve connections across more than 1,100 global trade lanes.

Other executives, like Jim Fitterling, executive chairman of chemicals giant Dow, defended Trump's tariffs despite their costs. Fitterling acknowledged they can drive up prices but argued they are necessary to combat unfair competition and protect U.S. manufacturing.

Democrats plan to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January, but his veto power and broad trade authority could make them difficult to overturn.

#2866. Hegseth impeachment effort torpedoed in House

The House of Representatives effectively torpedoed an effort to impeach Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth with a procedural vote on Tuesday evening.

The House, by a 211-204 margin, approved a measure that will refer the impeachment resolution to a committee.

That will leave little, if any, time for the committee and full House to act on it before Democrats take over the chamber and the Senate with a new majority in early January.

House Republican leadership had sought to kill the effort at impeachment, which was initiated Monday by Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky.

The vote came a week after the midterm elections, where Democrats trounced Republicans in the first congressional election since the Iran war broke out in February.

Hegseth had faced eight articles of impeachment.

Massie accused Hegseth of waging war in contravention of Congress, carrying out extrajudicial killings tied to boat strikes in the Caribbean against purported drug smugglers and suppressing free speech by targeting Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., a military veteran.

Massie is a libertarian who lost his Republican primary in May after clashing with President Donald Trump.

The Kentucky Republican led a bill last year to compel the release of files related to convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

Shortly after the vote, Trump said: "Democrats' attempt to impeach Secretary of War Pete Hegseth has miserably FAILED!"

"Every single Republican, except Thomas Massie, a major LOSER, voted in favor of Pete," the president said, declaring what he said was an "EXONERATION" of Hegseth.

A Republican forced the vote on Hegseth's impeachment. All Democrats voted against referring the measure to committee.

Defense Department spokesperson Jacob Bliss said earlier in the day that Hegseth "carried out direct, lawful orders from the President to secure the nation." The impeachment effort notably focused on Hegseth, not Trump, who is responsible for launching the various military operations referred to in the articles.

Hegseth, at a press conference at the Pentagon earlier Tuesday, railed against Massie and defended his leadership in the Iran war and other military operations.

"What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it's not accountability. It's a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment," Hegseth said

"It's a farce, and it's a stunt, and everybody knows it," Hegseth said. "It puts a target on the backs of American warriors by signaling that Congress will undermine their missions in real time."This is not patriotism. This is sabotage."

Trump, in a Truth Social post on Tuesday, said Hegseth "gets an 'A' PLUS, PLUS, PLUS for his defense against a disgruntled, angry, defeated, former Congressman, Thomas Massie, a TOTAL LOSER!"

Trump said Monday that he would stand by Hegseth even if the House impeached him. The president was impeached by the House two times during his first White House term. The Senate acquitted him in both cases.

Republicans hold a slim 218-214 majority in the House.

Some Republicans, notably including Vice President JD Vance, have directly blamed their midterm losses on the Iran war, which continues to see intermittent fighting amid an uneasy stalemate.

Neither of the two Cabinet secretaries impeached by the House has ever been removed by the Senate after a trial in that chamber.

#2864. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2863. BYU Remains in Top 10 of Latest College Football Playoff Rankings

The College Football Playoff committee dropped the latest rankings on Tuesday. After losing on Saturday, the BYU Cougars dropped just one spot to No. 10. Kalani Sitake's team received a clear message that their strength of schedule weighed heavily for the committee. This is due to two quality wins, including then-No. 2 Notre Dame.

BYU was one of four teams in the initial Playoff field that lost over the weekend. No. 1 Miami, No. 6 Kentucky and No. 10 Ole Miss were the other three. The Georgia Bulldogs took the top spot ahead of Week 11.

Even as a one-loss team, the SEC favorite has been given some grace due to three quality wins. Kirby Smart's team was just above the No. 2 Utah Utes, who pulled off a big win in the first-ever top 10 'Holy War'. They pulled in a sellout crowd in the Beehive State matchup last week and did it all in front of a national audience.

As it stands now, BYU could face a rivalry rematch against Utah in the Playoff Semifinal. It's wild to believe there could be an encore edition on the biggest national stage. We'll see if the predictions come true. There's also a slight chance the Cougars and Utes could meet in the Big 12 title game.

The Cougars are no longer in control of their playoff destiny. They just need to win out and could still end up being an at-large. If BYU does make the Big 12 championship game, they'll likely face Utah. This would require Texas Tech and Houston to suffer losses.

Not out of the realm of possibilities, but the odds are stacked against the Cougars right now. Keep in mind, BYU has been a two-loss team each of the past two seasons and still haven't made the Playoff.

No. 10 BYU hosts Baylor on Saturday (3:30 p.m. ET, FOX).

#2862. Anthropic opens Australian retail IPO offering as AI scrutiny intensifies

Anthropic, the maker of AI chatbot Claude, opened its Australian retail initial public offering on Wednesday, giving individual investors access to shares ahead of the company's planned Nasdaq listing.

The offering opened at 10 a.m. Australian Eastern Daylight Time on Wednesday and is expected to close at 5 p.m. on Nov. 17, according to the timetable published by participating broker Stake.

Anthropic is seeking to raise as much as $64.4 billion in its IPO, with a separate $26 billion private placement, bringing a combined total to as much as $90.4 billion. Shares are expected to be priced between $70 to $78 each.

The Australian retail offering is expected to consist of newly issued Class A common shares under a prospectus to be lodged with the Australian Securities and Investments Commission, Anthropic said on its website.

Participating brokers include Commonwealth Bank of Australia's CommSec unit as lead broker, along with Bell Potter, LGT Wealth, Macquarie Group, Morgans, Morgan Stanley Wealth Management, Sharesies and Stake.

Anthropic said the prospectus would be available on its IPO website once the Australian retail offering opens. Investors will need to review the document and apply through participating brokers under the terms of the offer.

Australia is one of the small numbers of markets outside the U.S. where retail investors will be able to buy the company's shares directly, alongside Japan, Canada and parts of Europe.

The San Francisco-based AI startup has applied to list its Class A common stock on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the ticker symbol "ANT." Anthropic said it does not plan to seek a listing on the Australian Securities Exchange.

The offering comes as Australia moves to tighten oversight of AI companies after an OpenAI agent gained unauthorized access to the medical statistics website of Medicare,  the country's universal health insurance program.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese said Tuesday that the government would introduce mandatory reporting for certain AI-related incidents and establish an AI safety regulator with powers to enforce penalties.

"The law needs to get ahead of, and in some cases, make sure that it catches up with what is a very fast-changing world," Albanese told Reuters.

The government is also considering changes to criminal law for cases involving serious misconduct.

The country has also ruled out a copyright-law exemption that would allow AI companies to train models on Australian content without licensing it.

Open AI and Anthropic told Australian parliament last month that they would support mandatory reporting of data breaches involving their AI agents, acknowledging that disclosure is currently voluntary.

#2861. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2860. U.S., China should ‘harbor no illusions’ as they cooperate, compete on AI, Bessent says

The U.S. and China should "harbor no illusions" about their differences as they engage to contain the risks of artificial intelligence, while competing with each other at the same time, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said.

Bessent said in prepared remarks to the National Committee on U.S.-China Relations in New York Tuesday that the two governments had established a new dialogue mechanism on "super intelligence," or SI, aimed at guarding against "shared risks" and averting any "misunderstanding between our two systems."

The mechanism will see both sides exchanging "best practices" for testing advanced models before they are released, and "keeping human judgement firmly in command of the technology," Bessent said, expecting both sides to alert each other to serious incidents involving the SI models.

Bessent, however, stopped short of making any commitment to limit advancement of the technology, as Beijing and Washington remain locked in an intense race to achieve global dominance in AI.

The Trump administration has largely resisted broadening restrictions on AI development over concerns of ceding a competitive edge to China, though the president has more recently signaled some openness to putting in place regulatory safeguards.

"I ​don't mind guardrails," he said in a CBS interview released Sunday, adding that ⁠he did not want regulations to put the U.S. in "a deep second position" behind China, ​which he ​said ⁠had no such restrictions.

China's foreign ministry in September dismissed calls to slow down frontier models, saying that fearmongering and confrontation would only disrupt global AI governance. Huiyao Wang, founder of Center for China and Globalization, a think tank that closely aligns with Beijing's thinking, said last month that the U.S.-China talks should "confront AI risks and manage them together."

During Xi's September visit, Washington and Beijing agreed to launch an AI dialogue with a communications channel to help defuse serious incidents. The two leaders are expected to meet for the third time next week, with Bessent joining, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation Leaders' Meeting from Nov. 18 to 19.

As the technology advances, the two nations will need to demonstrate how they can "reconcile the demands of competition with the responsibilities of leadership," Bessent said in his Tuesday speech. "Our interests will not always coincide, and our approach to every challenge likewise will not always converge," he added.

Washington had briefly suspended access by foreign nationals to Anthropic's Mythos and Fable 5 models in June before restoring it weeks later. The U.S. government has also restricted exports of the most advanced chips to Beijing, and accused Chinese developers of copying American models through what it calls industrial-scale distillation.

Xie Feng, China's ambassador to the U.S., told the audience at the gala that efforts by both countries were "indispensable" to addressing issues from global flashpoints to "improving artificial intelligence governance." Cooperation "may not resolve every challenge," he said, but "none can be resolved without it."

The event was organized at a time when controversy around Nvidia Corp CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy has been making headlines.

The billionaire tech executive, who joined Trump's delegation to Beijing in May and was invited to the state dinner in Washington in September, is at the center of a major corporate and national security scandal after admitting to an extramarital affair with a Chinese businesswoman whom the U.S. intelligence officials suspect of espionage.

While Huang's presence at the upcoming summit has been thrown into doubt, other business leaders such as Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk and Mastercard's Michael Miebach could be on Trump's delegation next week, according to South China Morning Post.

#2859. Koa Peat’s development requires patience amid Suns’ early struggles

You could feel it last night during the loss to the San Antonio Spurs, couldn't you? When Suns' rookie wing Koa Peat entered the game, you could feel that "disease of what if" beginning to creep in. Perhaps I felt it more than most because I'd spent my morning browsing eBay, looking through the newly released 2026-27 Topps basketball cards and hunting for Peat rookie cards. Side note: I love that they're doing the 45th anniversary of the 1981-82 Topps cards. Classically beautiful.

So when he entered the game with 5:56 left and the Suns trailing by 30 points in the fourth quarter, I suddenly had a reason to remain engaged for the rest of the evening. And he didn't disappoint. The first time Peat touched the ball, he attacked the cylinder, drove left, spun right, and banked a beautiful 6-foot shot off the glass for two points. On the very next possession, he found himself in transition and tossed an alley-oop to Michael Jordan reincarnated in Jamaree Bouyea for another two points.

I felt it deep within my soul. We need to get this guy minutes!

Then reality quickly set in. There aren't necessarily minutes available for him right now, and one of the primary reasons those two possessions looked so beautiful was sitting comfortably on the San Antonio bench. Victor Wembanyama, the very villain responsible for putting Phoenix in a 30-point hole, wasn't on the floor to swat either opportunity into the fifth row.

When you're watching a team that desperately lacks size and rebounding, any opportunity to see a player who possesses those attributes immediately feels like you've stumbled upon the solution. You begin convincing yourself that the coaching staff doesn't know what it's doing, that players like Peat should be receiving consistent opportunities because they offer something the current rotation is sorely missing.

On paper, that sounds wonderful. In reality, it's not nearly that simple, and there are legitimate reasons why it shouldn't happen right now.

For starters, where are those minutes supposed to come from? Ryan Dunn has been playing out of his mind to begin the season. Are we supposed to take some of his limited 16.7 minutes away? Do we cut his playing time in half so Peat can get on the floor long enough to work up a sweat without ever establishing enough rhythm to make those minutes meaningful?

Granted, if we could carve out some minutes from Jalen Green, I'd certainly entertain that conversation. Perhaps that's where an opportunity exists. But the reality is that developing chemistry and cohesion requires consistency within your rotations. You can't expect players to become comfortable with one another if you're constantly reshuffling responsibilities and playing time every time a rookie enters a game and strings together two positive possessions.

Quite honestly, if Peat is receiving significant minutes right now, it probably means the Suns have shifted their priorities away from winning in the present and toward developing for the future. As I've stated before, losing doesn't benefit this franchise. Phoenix doesn't control its own draft destiny, so intentionally prioritizing development at the expense of winning creates a situation where you're accumulating losses without receiving the traditional reward that comes with them. You might as well go out there and try to win basketball games.

There will eventually come a time when development becomes a greater priority. If the Suns remain 12th in the Western Conference standings as the season progresses, the organization may need to reevaluate its approach and provide more meaningful opportunities to its younger players. At that point, giving Peat, Fleming, and others additional minutes could become an important part of determining what the future looks like.

But we're nine games into the season. Nine. And while I recognize there are people already pounding their fists on the table demanding more playing time for Peat, I'm here to tell you that now is neither the time nor the place.

That's the challenge of the disease of what if. It sends your mind wandering toward hypothetical solutions that seem perfectly logical when you're watching your team get dismantled. You see a young player make a couple of exciting plays, recognize that he possesses attributes your team desperately needs, and begin imagining how differently things might have unfolded had he been given an opportunity earlier.

But countless variables contribute to those moments. The opponent's personnel, the score, the defensive intensity, the matchups, and the circumstances surrounding those opportunities all matter. And because you'll never receive a definitive answer to what would have happened had that player entered the game earlier, the hypothetical begins to feel like a solution.

Adding some fuel to the debate is that Suns' head coach Jordan Ott stated just a few days ago that Peat is "right on the cusp."

"Koa is next and Koa's right there on the tip," Ott stated. "We understand that he's young and he's hasn't done doesn't have a ton of NBA game experience, but again, the way the game is moving, the ability to drive, process, and defend the drive, he appears to have it, and he's right there. He'll get his opportunity, and we have full faith that that's what's coming."

The Suns could certainly use some help on the glass, as last night's loss served as another painful reminder of how difficult it has been for this team to secure rebounds. Phoenix was outrebounded by 20 against San Antonio and currently ranks dead last in the NBA with a 44.6% overall rebounding percentage. Perhaps even more concerning is its defensive rebounding percentage, which sits at a league-worst 60.1%. That means opposing teams have an alarmingly high likelihood of extending possessions by grabbing offensive rebounds, a recurring nightmare for Phoenix.

The consequences are showing up on the scoreboard. The Suns allow a league-high 19.0 second-chance points per game, essentially handing opponents additional scoring opportunities because they cannot consistently finish defensive possessions with a rebound. It's difficult enough to stop NBA offenses the first time around. When you're giving them multiple opportunities to score on the same possession, you're making an already challenging task nearly impossible.

Where do I land on Koa Peat? I want to see him play. I want to see what he can become, and I certainly want the Suns to develop the talent they've invested in. But wanting to see more of a player and believing he deserves more minutes right now are two different things.

The Suns have to balance the future with the present, and nine games into the season, they still have a responsibility to compete. Maybe Peat eventually earns a larger role. Maybe the circumstances change and create that opportunity. Until then, we have to resist the temptation to turn two exciting possessions into an answer to problems that are considerably more complicated. The disease of what if is powerful, but it doesn't necessarily make the prescription any better.

Me? I'll just head back over to eBay and hunt more rookie autos.

#2858. 32 grades on the 2026 NFL trade deadline: Who won, who lost and who did nothing?

The NFL trade deadline went down at 4 p.m. ET on Tuesday. Here's a breakdown of the trades and grades. Yahoo Sports' Eric Edholm assessed the NFC teams and Frank Schwab tackled the AFC. Trade activity is noted from late August:

NFC East: Cowboys | Giants | Eagles | Commanders NFC North: Bears | Lions | Packers | Vikings NFC South: Falcons | Panthers | Saints | Buccaneers NFC West: Cardinals | Rams | 49ers | Seahawks

AFC East: Bills | Dolphins | Patriots | Jets AFC North: Ravens | Bengals | Browns | Steelers AFC South: Texans | Colts | Jaguars | Titans AFC West: Broncos | Chiefs | Raiders | Chargers

CB Joey Porter Jr. (from Steelers, Oct. 1)

2027 seventh-round draft pick (from Texans, part of Nathan Thomas deal on Aug. 30)

OL Broderick Jones (from Steelers, Aug. 29)

2027 fourth-round draft pick (from Steelers, part of Broderick Jones deal on Aug. 29)

2028 second-round draft pick (to Steelers, part of Joey Porter Jr. deal on Oct. 1)

2027 sixth-round draft pick (to Steelers, part of Joey Porter Jr. deal on Oct. 1)

2027 third-round draft pick (to Steelers, part of Broderick Jones deal on Aug. 29)

2027 sixth-round draft pick, conditional (to Steelers, part of Broderick Jones deal on Aug. 29)

The Cowboys' big move came on Oct. 1 when they traded for CB Joey Porter Jr. for multiple picks. Looking back now, the move looks good from a value standpoint, as Jessie Bates III ended up being traded for more, and Porter has played pretty well for Dallas. The problem is that Porter has played in only four of Dallas' six games, and the Cowboys are still treading water. Team owner Jerry Jones hinted that the Cowboys were still big-game hunting in the trade market, but nothing else emerged.

QB J.J. McCarthy (from Vikings, Sept. 28)

2028 seventh-round draft pick (from Chiefs, part of Joshua Ezeudu deal on Aug. 26)

2027 fifth-round draft pick (to Vikings, part of McCarthy deal on Sept. 28)

2028 seventh-round draft pick (to Ravens, part of CJ Okoye deal on Aug. 29)

The Giants have had a fascinating pre-deadline period, acquiring J.J. McCarthy for nickels on the dollar and potentially changing the team's landscape at the position. Jaxson Dart presumably remains the 2027 starter, but a strong finish from McCarthy could set up some interesting optics next offseason. They were sellers before the draft, trading Dexter Lawrence to Cincinnati, but the Giants were shopping right up until the deadline as NFC East leaders. They reportedly went in hard on Jeffery Simmons and made the Colts a strong offer on Jonathan Taylor, but nothing materialized.

2027 first-round pick (from Jaguars, part of Jordan Davis deal on Nov. 9)

2029 second-round pick (from Jaguars, part of Jordan Davis deal on Nov. 9)

2028 second-round draft pick (from Ravens, part of Cam Jurgens deal on Oct. 7)

2027 fifth-round draft pick (from Ravens, part of Cam Jurgens deal on Oct. 7)

2027 seventh-round draft pick (to Ravens, part of Cam Jurgens deal on Oct. 7)

It has been a surprising turn of events with the Eagles — often among the more aggressive teams around deadline time — turning into sellers. Philadelphia dealt A.J. Brown this offseason but also traded for multiple players, so that felt like a separate situation. The in-season loss of Jalen Hurts has precipitated some of this movement, but the Eagles were already in sell mode with the Cam Jurgens trade the week prior to Hurts being diagnosed with blood clots. Trading Davis brought back some potentially great returns. It's not how we imagined it going, but the Eagles were smart to pivot, especially Hurts' murky future.

The Commanders were reportedly open to the idea of trading DT Daron Payne, whose contract expires after this season, but he suffered a broken foot, thus killing off any deal. The same might have been true for Javon Kinlaw, who is also on IR. The team's salary

cap situation is fine, but the Commanders are underwater at 2-5-1 and could use the draft-pick help. They've made only 11 selections combined the past two drafts, with a roster that needs an influx of young talent.

DT Jeffery Simmons (from Titans, Oct. 29)

2028 fifth-round draft pick (from Titans, part of Jeffery Simmons deal on Oct. 29)

CB Clark Phillips III (from Falcons, Aug. 30)

2027 fifth-round draft pick (from Falcons, part of Gervon Dexter Sr. deal on Aug. 30)

2027 first-round draft pick (to Titans, part of Jeffery Simmons deal on Oct. 29)

2028 first-round draft pick (to Titans, part of Jeffery Simmons deal on Oct. 29)

2027 fourth-round draft pick (to Titans, part of Jeffery Simmons deal on Oct. 29)

DT Gervon Dexter Sr. (to Falcons, Aug. 30)

It's possible we look back at the Simmons trade as the most impactful of the 2026 cycle. In Simmons' first game as a Bear, he took over against the Seahawks, giving the team and its fans hope for another Super Bowl run. Two first-round picks is potentially a significant cost for a defensive tackle, but if the Bears finish closer to the top of the standings, those picks might be worth about the same as the No.10 overall selection the Bengals paid for Dexter Lawrence. The Simmons trade was aggressive and could help put the Bears over the top. What's not to like?

2028 sixth-round draft pick (to Jets, part of Andre Cisco deal on Nov. 9)

The Lions were reportedly one of the first teams to inquire about Joey Porter Jr., and when he went to Dallas, Lions fans were not too happy with general manager Brad Holmes. The Lions have added safety depth, acquiring Cisco from the Jets. Their defensive concerns likely remain, even with stronger play of late, but Holmes resisted making any big moves.

RB Kaleb Johnson (from Steelers, Aug. 30)

2028 sixth-round draft pick (from Dolphins, part of Kyle McCord deal on Aug. 30)

2028 sixth-round draft pick (to Steelers, part of Kaleb Johnson deal on Aug. 30)

2028 seventh-round draft pick (to Rams, part of Mark Redman deal on Aug. 30)

The Packers started the season 1-2 but have ripped off five wins in their past six games and are emerging as a sneaky title contender, especially with Micah Parsons on his way back. That likely will have to count Green Bay's big "acquisition" this season, although they did reportedly kick the tires on Jonathan Taylor amid Josh Jacobs' once-uncertain status. Do the Packers have enough to make a big run? We'll find out.

2027 fifth-round draft pick (from Giants, part of J.J. McCarthy deal on Sept. 28)

2027 sixth-round draft pick (from Patriots, part of Walter Rouse deal on Aug. 30)

2027 third-round draft pick (to Raiders, part of Cousins deal on Nov. 10)

2027 seventh-round draft pick (to Patriots, part of Walter Rouse deal on Aug. 30)

The Vikings shipped out McCarthy to the Giants, and it seemed to backfire initially with McCarthy showing early promise in New York and Carson Wentz struggling against the Bills in place of an injured Kyler Murray. That prompted the Vikings to bring Kirk Cousins back to Minnesota, where he played for head coach Kevin O'Connell previously, in a desperate attempt to provide offensive stability. The Vikings have a world-class defense, and it would be criminal not to give that unit a fighting chance to compete for a Super Bowl. We'll see how quickly Cousins can re-integrate into the system, but a third-round pick likely means they're serious about him starting quickly.

sixth-round draft pick (from Ravens, part of Ryan Neuzil deal on Nov. 10)

2027 second-round draft pick (from Jaguars, part of Jessie Bates III deal on Nov. 9)

2028 fifth-round draft pick (from Jaguars, part of Jessie Bates III deal on Nov. 9)

DT Gervon Dexter Sr. (from Bears, Aug. 30)

CB Clark Phillips III (to Bears, Aug. 30)

2027 fifth-round draft pick (to Bears, part of Gervon Dexter Sr. deal on Aug. 30)

You can say the Falcons were technically in a divisional playoff race when they dumped Jessie Bates III for draft picks. On the other hand, you can also reasonably understand the thinking of the trade when you consider the terrific value Atlanta received and also that the Falcons likely were more fool's-gold contenders. They'll have a good allotment of draft picks heading into what should be a significant offseason for the franchise.

DT TeRah Edwards (from Chargers, Aug. 30)

2028 sixth-round draft pick (to Chargers, part of TeRah Edwards deal on Aug. 30)

The Panthers spent big on two defensive pieces this offseason, signing Jaelan Phillips and Devin Lloyd for big money, and they've mostly rested on their laurels since. The results have been underwhelming, and Carolina sits at 3-5. That's miraculously tied for first place. The Panthers are contenders, but they're not overextending themselves for a big run at the NFC South crown.

2028 sixth-round draft pick (from Jaguars, part of Davon Godchaux deal on Nov. 9); could elevate to a 2027 sixth-round pick

The Saints' first pre-deadline move was to ship out Godchaux to the Jaguars for a future Day 3 pick. It was not the kind of inspiring move some fans hoped for after a dramatic win over the Browns, keeping the Saints in the divisional hunt. Realistically, the Saints were always more likely to be sellers than buyers, with limited upside this season.

Like most NFC South teams, the Buccaneers had been more connected to selling than buying. Vita Vea's name popped up in some trade rumors in the past week, with the Giants reportedly among the teams interested, but nothing materialized after Vea signed a one-year, $30 million extension in August. And despite the Bucs needing some skill-position help on offense, they apparently were willing to wait until the offseason for any major additions.
